379 he is going to be. We have nothing to fear for he is different from any young man I know. He sees the principle behind everything and it does not take him long to let you know he does. When he gets before the Judge and a Jury his opponent will have to be careful. I am longing to hear his first case. He fears no man. Who would have thought it possible that he could be such a man after his breakdown?" "Breakdown nothing, his coming up you mean.

He always was positive, but we tried to subdue him. 

We are complete failures and he is a huge success- :so far." "Mother, you do not know half of what he is doing. The other day a young father came to him on the street and asked him for enough money to buy a loaf of bread. He looked at this young father and said, 'what is wrong, too lazy to work for it?' He saw rage and insult rise up within him as he lifted his hand ready to strike. Our son smiled put his hand in his pocket and gave the man a dollar; then said, 'take me to your home, I want to see it.' He took him there. There were three little children, a wife sick with hunger and the children crying for bread. He took bread and milk home. Such a sight! The man lived in the same street as one of the men who prayed that the poor might be fed.

After our son saw the conditions, he asked what his trade was. He told him; then he said, 'come with me.' He took him to the office-not to the man who hires; but the head office and told them the story. 

He said, 'not to play upon your sympathy but to measure your manhood. How much of a man are you? Show me.' He got him employment. This story is circulated all over town; yet he never men-

tioned it to us. He is a boy to be proud of even if we do not understand. I wonder where he will end?" 

They will have to wait and see.

He walks up the hill. When he arrives, he sees he is there first and that pleases him. He has found her there twice, now he is waiting for her. Will she come? Does she understand? He waits until he grows impatient and walks to the crest of the hill. 

He looks down at the footpath and sees her approaching. She looks up and sees him. She hastens her steps and with a smile says, "So you are waiting for me? I thought night would never come." "Yes, and I thought you would never come." "Why, you have been here not more than fifteen minutes. 99 "Is that all? How do you know?" "Never mind, that is my secret." "All right, I would not take your secret; but I have my own thoughts."

He takes her hand and they sit together for a long time. They do not speak. Why? Because love is too much for words; so they fill up to the overflowing. She breaks the silence by saying, "Life! 

What is life?" "Life is just what you make it," he says, "just as you see it, just as you live it. How many have lived yet never lived. At this moment, I can say I have lived and I live as I never lived before. To be with you once more makes something swell up within and these words come, 'a love so true and gentle fills a place in my life that would have been but a myth if I had not met you and knew that you loved just as I know that my love is all yours. My love you may have so my life can go on to a higher plane,

MEASURING MAN 

381 the plane where you dwell. I know that love will hasten me on to do greater things as time goes on. 

You will be an ideal so far above me that it will take me ages to come up to you. I will give you the key to my treasure chest of love and you only can open the door to my fountain of love. Your smile will be a beacon to me and I will trod on the path of Wisdom with you. When I prove my manhood in life 

I want you to know it will be just for you as my wife.

We were put together by the Creator. You see, man's laws in marriage mean nothing to me. I will live to please only you and then I know my Creator will see the Circle of love He gave you and I.'" "You seem to take it for granted that I am yours.

Such a peculiar way you have of asking me." "I am not asking you, you have nothing to say for I know my Creator made it that way and you know we cannot go against Him for He saw us as we are so we must not spoil His Creation." "As you will. No, as we will." "That is better. I am satisfied, are you? Of course, we will not get together until I have reached my goal in life and when I am ready to make that goal complete then I will ask you to come and finish the picture so we can journey on-both painting the same picture at one time; and the name of it will be 'Happiness' with a wreath around it with the words 'As He Saw So We Are."" "How wonderful you are," she says. "How much more wonderful I will be when two wonders become one. How wonderful we will be. 

What, are you crying?" "No, only tears of joy, the over-flow of the woman's fountain of love that cannot be expressed in

words. When we trod the path together and we come to this journey's end, how glad we will be to start on another life again. I hope we will not separate for life without you would not be life. Let us work together and give of our light to the world that we might forward the time when man will see that it is a man's job to live-if he would live the life of a man."

The night speeds on, time to these two means nothing. They arise to go, she suggests that he visit her home. "You mean for me to ask your parents' consent?" "Yes." "No, your word is enough for me. I am not asking them for anything. I am taking that which belongs to me and that is you-you only-not your parents. 99 "All right, have your way. You seem to see life differently from anyone I have ever met; but it suits me. When will I see you again?" "Not until I come back from college." "I was wondering why you should have to go to college when you know more than anyone else." "You see it is the law of our state that we must spend so much time there and so much money; then whether you know anything or not, whether you know the law, or memorize it enough to satisfy a certain class of men, you can become a lawyer. To me, it is a joke but a serious one and a waste of time. Since we are living in an age of Education, we have to at least make them believe they are teaching us something worthwhile; and since we have to pay for it, why not enjoy it. I do not have to think, study or memorize which gives me time to

MEASURING MAN 

383 look into the principle of the college. It gives me a chance to read the man behind the man who apparently knows so much. I study the students and watch them go through the stage of what they call 'seeking to be something different to the masses,' making themselves believe they are aloof when in reality they are just servants of the masses: for you know if the masses committed no error they would be men without an income. It shows me that there isn't any man who is independent. We all depend upon the other man for what we eat; so really that makes life a circle also and all the people pass money around in circles-no one having the right to live upon himself. Is it not wonderful to realize no matter what position you aspire to and reach you have to depend upon the masses for a living. We are all servants of the others. Why and how they claim profession and position and make themselves believe they are so important in the world, I cannot understand; but it is interesting and worth watching. It gives you a glance into real life just as real life should be. There is no man independent, neither is there anyone selfmade. And any man who thinks he is-well, he is a dreamer of the impossible. There I go again; yet what would I have been today if I had not met that Master-mind?”" "Have you seen him since we met here?" "No, I seldom see him; yet when I need him, he seems to come. I was wondering today when he would be passing this way again. You know I get lonesome to see him." "So do I. He always gives us a new sight into life when we do meet him-enough to keep us going for a long time. One time I asked him why we see

him so seldom, his reply was: 'never over-feed anything for if you give one bite too many, it takes the taste away from what you have already eaten and enjoyed; so I never over-feed neither do I let anything get too hungry.' 999 "No, to me that is not so. Every day, as I travel, he feeds me by the things he told me I would see. 

Everything I see in Creation is a new food; so I am satisfied to wait for him. He will not fail us." "You surely have great confidence in him." "Confidence nothing. By him I live that I might see him again. Tomorrow, I go again to meet the men of last year and the young men of this year. 

It is getting late. Time goes so quickly when you are with someone who interests you, does it not?" "Yes, it just flies as on wings; but we have something to be thankful for and that is although we are about to part again in the flesh our thoughts will be as one, for we do travel on the same road. We will live in wonderful anticipation of our next meeting. Will you write to me?" "I am sorry to say, I will not for that would spoil the joy of our meeting the next time. I would tell you everything in letters and would have to repeat it all over again when we met so as to have something to talk about; so we will accumulate everything until we meet again. We will close the safety valves and keep the pressure intact; then we will have much to talk about. Let us return to the world once more. You may tell your parents of our future together so you will not have to keep it a secret for I do not believe in keeping such good news. 

Let them be happy to know that you have found something too great to keep."

MEASURING MAN 

385 "What about your parents?" "They need not know. It is a man's business, in my case-not a daughter's affair."

As they say Good-night, they leave already in the eyes of their Creator 'two in one.' What a picture in life worthwhile!

CHAPTER IX  

The next day he leaves for college. 

He again is welcomed by his Professor and the collegians.

The season passes with him in his own way interpreting laws as they never had been understood before.

As the session closes he has again merited high honors and strange to say there is no one jealous of his superiority. It is natural for him to be so and they look at it as such.

Once more he is on his way home. He has not yet decided what train of investigation he will follow. He has been offered position after position; but he refuses them all. He arrives home and is welcomed by his parents. Once more they ask him to stay home and take a rest, his father tells him he is their only son and heir and all they have will be his; so why can he not stay at home with them that summer. "I am old enough and strong enough and know enough to make my own living; then why should I live from the money you have earned? If you have enough, why don't you retire from the field of labor and reap your harvest? You can rest, travel and see life as it should be seen. Spend in your life what you have saved. Do not leave anything for me. I will be able to support myself. I will never live on the

earnings of another-not if I have to starve. When 

I cannot work, I will not eat; so you see, Dad, your money means nothing to me. Look at this in the right light and you will see the true meaning of my words. You have been a good father, I have had a kind mother and I, as a man, appreciate both; but do not spoil your son who has set a goal in life and is determined to reach that goal, no matter what befalls his path. I will, in a day or two, make up my mind what I want to do this summer. This will be my last summer of freedom. Next year I will be a full-fledged lawyer." At this he smiles. "I must go out. I will see you tomorrow."

He ascends the hill which means so much to him and as he nears his shrine, there is someone waiting for him. They meet, each with a light of love in their eyes; but neither speaks a word. They sit in silence for a long time then he says, "Well, has life been good to you ?" "I do not know about life; but my thoughts have been wonderful. I am proud to be as I am to you.

The papers are full of your greatness today. I would not be surprised if the city gives you a special day. 

The whole city is proud of you; then how do you think I should feel?" "Oh, the masses make much ado about nothing. 

I did not do anything great this year nor did I say anything great that I knew of. The speech I made was so simple and easy.' 

"Yes, but how much you said in that speech. Do you remember the things you said? "No, how could I? I just got up and said a few words then sat down and wondered why they applauded so. It seemed all so ridiculous to me. As

I have heard somewhere the saying, 'much ado about nothing.' Wait until I am a lawyer and have the right to speak then I may say something worth the saying." "I knew you were too big to see your own bigness. 

How proud I am of you!" "Show it then. Do not sit there and say these things; but show me by acting just what you think of me." "Do you think that is fair to me? Is it a woman's place to take the initiative ?" 

He smiles. "I never thought of it that way. How stupid I am."

When they could talk again, she reads his speech to him by the aid of a flashlight. He listens. When she finishes, he says, "Is it possible that I said all this? I thought I just talked five minutes, and I declare, it took you over half an hour to read it. Can it be possible I talked that length of time? How I must have bored them." "Do you think they would applaud the way they did for boring them? No, but for the beautiful thoughts you gave them. For the food for thought you fed them. They will clamor for more. The papers will ask you for more and they will offer to pay you well to write for them. Just wait and see." 

The night speeds on as all night do when life is worthwhile. They walk home together and seem to walk on air. Love like this lifts you from the solidity of the Earth. You walk without the knowledge of walking only the journey is too short. 

They say Good-night once more. She asks when she will see him again. "It all depends on the morrow and what I find

to do; whether I may have to leave the city or not. 

I will let you know. Good-night.”

He leaves her and says to himself, "Love is wonderful when it rises from the truth of the knowledge of the word. A word so small yet means so much in life. Without this love, what a cold meaningless world it would be. That picture is too dark for me. 

I am thankful there is love and that love I have found my portion in its purity." Full of these thoughts he retires with a new feeling. Every day reveals its own gift to him. As he feels himself falling asleep, his Soul-mind whispers, "Greater things than these will be revealed unto thee. Keep to the path of Wisdom."

The next morning he is up early, ready to go out in the world. He appears at the breakfast table and meets his parents with his smiling "Good-morning."

He looks for their smile in return, but it fails to appear; so he asks, "Why are you so sad that you cannot smile?" "You will never understand," says the mother, "until you have an only son and love that son so much that he is the light, the type of love, the aspiration to live and succeed and prepare for his future life and happiness; then when you could fulfill all the anticipations of years, those anticipations are shattered by that son never being at home, never being able to do anything for him, feeling him slipping into a stranger to the ones who idolized him, hear everyone talking of his greatness but cannot have the privilege of listening to the things he says to others. Son, it is a mother's heart speaking. If I have hurt you in any way, please forgive me. I am just a mother." She finishes with tears in her eyes.

He looks at her, sees and understands. He goes to her and says, "Mother, I do love you and how

I have tried to show my worthiness to such a mother. 

You wanted me to be big; but in your own way.

I wanted to be big in my own way. We drifted apart; but this morning we will come to an understanding and that is, I love you and I always have and long for you to put your arms around me as I have seen other mothers do; but you never did and 

I was afraid to force my love upon you. What a misunderstanding! For what?-For the fear of what the masses might think. Mother, from now on, no matter where we are, no matter who is there, when I want to put my arms around my mother, I am going to do it, whether you like it or not, for after all you are my mother." He puts his arms around her and gives her the first real son's kiss for many years. The tears come forth from the subdued fountain of a mother's love. A new mother is born to her son. 

As the father looks at this scene, he, too, swells with emotion, stands up and says, "Son, what a lesson in life you have given me this morning. I knew 

I had a wonderful son only by what people say of you. Now I know I have a son, indeed.-Only I think you are not fair to us. You give us so much, yet you refuse to let us do the least thing for you.

As your mother said, until you have a son as we have and he becomes just what you have been, then and only then will you understand a father and mother such as we are today. If we had other children, it might not have been so hard; for what you have refused we could have given to the others. 

You are our all, yet you will take nothing from us.

I hope you will never be in our position. It is too hard to understand."

As his father says these words, the son goes to him and says, "I am proud of such a father; and since mother and you told me of your feelings this morning, the Silent Voice said, 'stay with your parents in your own city. You can still meet men of different character, men of different position, nay, men in almost every line of life. Stay home, meet these people and you will be well repaid.' You see, Father, before you and mother finished your breakfast, the

Silent Voice of my Soul-mind spoke to me and my summer at home is assured." 

The eyes of his mother shine with a new love.

The father's eyes sparkles with an inward joy; and the three leave the breakfast room understanding each other in the natural, the unnatural being thrown away for at least this short time until each sees the other in his true worth. A home for three for the first time since this little "Bud of Humanity" appeared. What a revelation! The father never left for his office in such spirit as he does this morning. 

The mother never carried the dishes to the kitchen with such vigor. The boy smiles the smile of truth as he breathes a few silent words. "A mother's love, a father's pride should make any man face the tide and fight against the odds in life to make himself a man within himself." 

Each starts the day in his own way. Each heart is singing a new song such as never has been in this home before. This has been what the world would have called a Home; but up until today it has just been a House to leave and come back to-not the young man's fault nor the parent's fault. He is an

advanced Soul-mind, his parents of the material; but today it is a Home. Let us hope they continue to understand each other in the future and that their Home may be a Home of reality. 

When he leaves his home this morning, he has no set purpose in view. The voice has spoken so let the voice lead him. He goes into the city. He thinks he will spend the morning in the station and watch the human, natural picture that can only be seen where the passers-by are unconscious of observation. He takes his seat to watch the stage of life being acted in its true picture. He spends an interesting morning. He looks and sees hundreds of people; yet how very few seem to be happy or contented in life. 

They appear to him to be a moving mass of Human Clay with no goal ahead, no anticipation of life worthwhile. Some seem to be satisfied, some even seem to smile or laugh as they pass him by. Another picture comes before him. As he glances around at the people who are waiting, almost everyone is reading. He sees this and the Silent Voice says to him, "Yes, the world today is seeking the line of least resistance. Everyone you see reading is reading the thoughts of some other man. It is so much easier to borrow knowledge than it is to seek it for yourself. Why? Because Education teaches the principle of the line of least resistance, therefore, it is not the fault of the masses, but the laws of Education that retard the progress of the natural mind. 

If Education would reveal the principle only; then let the pupil work it out for himself, we would have more original thought today. Originality is the foundation of life in its true form." 

As he sits and has these things revealed to him,

time, speeds so fast, that he is surprised when he sees it is time for dinner. He walks back home and thinks: What a morning, what a thought, what a picture of the material-yes, material-not natural for the natural could not give a picture so sad. It is the unnatural putting, unconsciously, the natural play on the stage of life. 

When he reaches home, dinner is ready. His father asks, "How did you spend your morning, Son?" "Watching the play we call 'life' on the stage of the real, where the actors were all in their natural state, not being conscious of an audience. They had to put on no air or camouflage. Dad, it was an interesting picture. It showed every side of life in every line of life in the line of manhood. I could see position from what the people call big down to the man who wanted to clean my shoes for a nickle; yet they are all part of the Creator's Supreme Creation. To see man at the beginning, each with his two sides of life and his free will that the Creator gave him in the beginning, to either become great or small, high in thought or low in thought, a will to resist or submit to the line of least resistance. When 

I saw that picture this morning, it seemed to me not less than 85% of the human race have succumbed to the life of the line of least resistance. 

Why, Father, do you think this is so?" 

His father looks at him in surprise. "Son, I must admit, I have sat in stations for hours during my life, but I never saw the picture you saw today. I never gave the scene a thought. I am afraid I cannot answer your question. You see, we have been taught we must have rich and we must have poor

and also a few in the world such as I who am neither rich nor poor but have enough to see us through so we are satisfied to set up and look down upon the poor." 99 The son says, "The same as the rich sit up and look down upon us. The poor, to me, are in a more peculiar position; and if analyzed rightly, they have the better of us. They have nothing to look down upon so are obliged to look up; and as they do, I wonder what they think they are looking up to? 

Their masters? No, their servants, only a little better paid in money than they are. Yet, after all, the masses are the masters of the rich. The rich today think they are independent; but are they?

If the masses were to say tomorrow, 'we will not work, but we will eat,' how soon the rich would be rich no more: for only by the demands of the necessities of life do the producers get rich through the labor of the masses." "Son, you surely see life in its truth; but I am afraid, if you speak your thoughts freely, you will get into disfavor with the class of people you will meet in the line of life you have chosen." "As I see so shall I speak. As the picture comes to me so will I present it to the people. If the truth hurts let them leave the man of truth and follow the man of make-believe. I will never be afraid to speak my true convictions before any man.

If I feel I should not then I will hold my peace. I have made up my mind that I will never start a conversation nor will I enter into an argument nor join in a conversation already started on any issue. I must know the principle, so you see, Dad, if I say anything to those people you talk about, they will

have to ask for what they get from me. If they ask for it and I give it to them, whether it suits or not, they asked for it, why not let them have it?" "Son, you are too deep for me, you make me wonder." "Wonder what?" "How a man so young as you can talk as you do, no loop-hole, no come-back, no trying to cover up, no trying to smooth things over. If you go on, no man will be your master. I can see that." "I will stand for no man to be my master in the things material; but I must admit, I have a master mind above me and I am proud that he condescended to pick me out of the masses, out of the cog wheel of industry, out of the line of least resistance and showed me a man, yes, a man. He said 'you, too, can if you will, become, not the master of men but the light to the advanced path that may lead others to follow you on the path of Wisdom: For as you rise so shall they have to look up to see you. As they see and hear you speak as few men speak, they will see in you a master also. Not a master to subdue, but a master to lift you up, a master who will forget the small things you do and will praise you and give you encouragement for the big things you do.' He does this for what? Money? No. I asked him what his price was to lead me on and his answer was: 'do thy duty. Fulfill the trust the Creator put in thee and you will have paid me abundantly. I seek not after riches but Wisdom.'" "Son, when did you see this strange man last?

I have never forgotten the things he told me when 

I met him." "I do not know, Dad. It is over a year since I

saw him last, in the flesh; but I feel him near me so often that I expect to meet him any time now. He always told me when I needed him he would be there." "He must be very interesting." "Yes, Dad, he talks Wisdom only; and makes everything so plain that it really makes me wonder where he acquired such Wisdom." in "One-thirty P.M. I never had such a dinner hour my life. Can it be possible time could fly so quickly? Mother, give me another cup of tea, this is ice cold. I never neglected my tea in my life before.” 

How pleased that mother is to serve this second cup of tea; for is it not the conversation of her son that made his father forget his tea? He drinks it down, puts on his hat and away he goes. Do his feet hit the pavement? They do; but if you tell him he will not believe you. He is walking on air and his thoughts are: "What a son!" His eyes are lighted up with a father's unsurpassable joy. What a son!

These words are still with him as his clients come into his office. He listens to their stories; but above it all, he wants to tell them of his son, of the things he has said; but dare he? No, or he would lose all his practice in a short time. So he holds his peace. 

Why? Because he is of the material and is afraid of his clientage and the people. Man, I wonder?

After the father leaves, the mother says, "My boy, how I have misunderstood you. What a vacant past. Can you ever forgive me?" "Let us not talk of the past, the picture is too sad to remember. Let us look to the future and a truer understanding of what a mother should be to a son and what a son should be to a mother." He

puts his arms around her and draws her close to his heart and says as he looks down into her eyes, "Mother, I have always loved you; but I had, I see it now, fear; and I thought or was taught it was respect. Today I do not fear you, the word respect is not big enough, the only word that can be used is: 

Mother I love you with the love a son should have for a mother."

Again he leaves home to spend the afternoon watching the stage and upon it the play of life. He is wondering where he should go and walks without, apparently, seeing ahead. At this moment a car pulls up to the curb and a voice brings him back to the world again. He looks up to see who it was and recognizes one of his college mates. The young man in the car asks him, "What are you going to do this summer?" "Stay home and live." "What do you call this, Life? I don't, I call this killing time. I am going up to the golf links to loaf. How would you like to take a ride? I would like to ride with someone who can live in the summer time." "All right, I would like to ride with someone who is killing time. To me, time is too short. I can never get enough in in one day; but since I saw the play on the stage of the masses, it might be interesting to see the play of the upper circle. I want to see life as it really is then some day I may put a play on the stage before the world and show the play of life as it should be." "What are you going to be, a reformer?" "No, just try to show people that life is too interesting to waste time-that is all."

"So you still live by the principle you spoke of to me two years ago? Do you know I have never forgotten the conversation we had then. I wish I could forget. It comes before me when I let myself go and am going to have a good time; then I see the ridiculous side of it just as you showed me that mental picture. The first thing I know I am sitting alone and that picture is before me and, sure enough, they do seem to be just what you said, 'pieces of human clay' seeking someone to remodel them and as they are modeled so they are. It makes me afraid to make love to any of them; for I have tried it. 

As sure as I do, they begin molding right away; then your words come to me, yes, just as strong as the day you said them: 'be sure the sculptor you give your clay to is a highly talented, efficient and lovable person; for only by greatness of the molder can the mold be made worthy of the sculptor. Be sure she can see you for what you are worth at present as a man, then you will increase in value as she molds you into what she wants you to be; for women can either make or mar.' You see, I have a good memory. Is that not word for word the way you spoke to me?" 

Our young Master smiles as the thoughts come to him. They are the words of his strange Mastermind: "Give your conversation in mental pictures and they will not have to memorize for they can never forget. As sure as they see the part of that picture that stands out before them so sure will all of the pictures once more come before them." 

His companion says, "You know as soon as a young lady comes into my life, she brings that picture with her."

He smiles and says, "When the right artist comes along, you will forget the picture. You will be so interested in the molding.' 99 "Here we are at the golf links. I wish it had been miles away. You are so interesting, I never heard anyone talk like you. From where do you get such wonderful thoughts?" "From the book of Creation." "Where can you get this book?" "Oh, it is everywhere. It is not a book written by man. The Creator wrote it himself. You can see the leaves turning at every minute of the day and every day of your life. If you lived one thousand years and read this book every moment of that time you would only be at the beginning. You see, as you read, it turns its leaves. This morning I saw a page of Humanity in its every stage from the richest to the poorest, from the most intelligent down to the dullest intellect. I saw people fed with everything good to eat, with costly clothes on; then again I saw others who showed hunger in their faces, sadness in their eyes and whose clothes were filthy, dirty and hardly covered their bodies. I saw some hurrying with somewhere to go and some just walking as you said this morning 'killing time'only these men and women were willing to work but could not find it, therefore, they were seemingly going nowhere. Yes, the page this morning was a picture worth reading; but it makes you wonder what the Creator thinks if He can look down upon His Creation and see this picture even as I see it. It is almost too sad a picture to look upon. I know He did not will this; but by giving man a free will He

can only look on, for man, only, made this unequality not He." "Then you think the Creator, as you name Him, has nothing to do with the lives of the people; that 

He did not give one man more thinking power than the other; that He has not chosen some to be rich and others to be poor; that He did not call certain men forth to fill high positions in the world? Why we have been taught to believe this ever since man could think enough to write history. Do you condemn and deny that Kings were chosen, that ministers and priests were not called, that other great leaders of Nations, present and past, were given the gift of leadership by the Creator? Can it be possible that these things that have been taught us are only the master-mind predominating over the lesser mind so they might keep the masses in fear and call it 'respect,' so that they might select them to become their servants and slaves in industry, that the few might live in comfort and luxury? Do you mean that you do not believe in this we have been taught to believe, and that you are big enough to declare yourself before the rich and the prosperous and tell the priests and ministers that they were not called by the voice of the Creator? Are you going to be big enough to tell all this to the world? You inspire me even as I quote. As it comes to me, I seem to feel, I seem to see that what you will say is true; but how is the world going to take it? Will the masses believe you? Will the rich believe you? Will you be able to convince these people that claim divinity that they are not divine or never could be? Why, if the world will see this truth, what a world we will have."

"Yes, but wait, it is now time I have my say or my reader will wonder what manner of young man this is that you are talking to. I will answer all your questions. I will explain in few words just what 

Creation's book says of these things. It says the 

Creator is just and has caused everything that man may need to be put in His store house for the benefit of man-only He made it that man must labor to live, for by the fruits of his labor only can he live; so you see, since He saw enough for all, He called man forth. He did not create anyone on a higher level than the other. If He did, He would be showing partiality; and can a Creator be just if He showed the least favor to any of His Creation? 

Therefore, you see, the unequality we see in the world is man's distribution-not the Creator who could not be other than just. Where is this difference we are told of? Do some men have something that the masses have not? If they do, I fail to see it. Take a man from the highest position and a man well kept and intelligent from the masses. 

Undress them both, groom them alike, set them side by side, call a stranger forth, yes, an intelligent stranger, and ask him to tell us which is the called or chosen and which is of the masses. "Could he?" "I am afraid not." "Then if man can not detect the difference in them naturally; it takes the artificial to let man see the contrast-so the contrast is in the clothing only and not in the Creator's part at all. "That is an answer worthy of consideration. 

Wonderful! Yes, I left home to kill time; you, as you said, to live. Would that I could live, see life and understand it as you do; then, I, too, could say

truly as you do 'I live.' Here is where you will see another leaf in your book of Creation, but not today, the day is gone. I never saw one of them leave, but there is not one left. What a day! What a lesson in life! What a picture stored away again for future reflection !" "I, too, have enjoyed the afternoon."

They turn their car. A friendship that can never be broken has been welded once more by the hand of time pointing each young man to the same spot at the same time.

At home that night he has not much to say. His mind is on a certain hill and on that hill he will be, if someone will be there; for he feels the call of the peace and quietness he finds there. It was his shrine of silence once; but it is his shrine of love and peace now. After supper he leaves once more not to see the stage of the play of life but to be an actor himself on the stage in the play we call 'love.' I often write this word and sometimes

I wonder if anyone else gets as much as I do in this little word that rules the world, this little word that either makes a man or a monkey at its will. It turns the ruler into a servant; it turns a lion into a lamb; it lifts up and it destroys; it brings unspeakable joy, yet sometimes it brings the tortures of hell; it builds up castles, then it tears down lives of men; it makes men brave, then it makes cowards of thousands of men; it brings the sweetest music, yet at times brings the sounds of war. Yes, love rules the world, yet how few of us see it that way. It has been the cause of many a broken heart, and sealed the lips of many who dare not tell; but could live in that land of secret love. It is better to have loved in secret than

not to have loved at all. A life without love is a heart without a beat or a life without a Soul-. It is not life, only killing time and time is too precious to waste. As these thoughts pass through his mind, he is nearing his place of silence. Is she there? Yes, just as he felt she would be.

Tonight, we will not listen in or try to take the place of either, so we will wait until the morrow.

CHAPTER X  

The next morning at the breakfast table, the father asks the son if he would like to spend the morning in the office with him. "I have an interesting case coming before me and I would like very much if you would go." "Yes, Dad, I will be pleased to go with you, if I will be permitted to stay and listen to them as they give their sides of the case. Do you have the winning side or the losing side, the guilty side or the innocent side?" "As yet, I cannot say; but this morning will decide that. That is why I would like you to hear the outline of this case. There is a large sum of money involved; so, no matter what side we represent, we will have a very large fee." 

The son looks at his father and the father sees a light in his son's eyes that he has never seen before. "Father, as I see this case, even if you have the innocent to defend or the guilty to try to prove innocent, you will fight the case for the fee and the fee alone. I will go and hear this case. It will be a picture of the future life you have planned for me. 

How glad I am that I am big enough to make my own plans in the material things in life." 

As he sits and listens in his father's office, the words, "Seek Wisdom and all things shall be revealed unto thee," come strong to him and he seeks

to see whether these people are the guilty or the innocent. As he looks at one of the people, the voice says, "Open thine eyes and thou shall see wonderful things." His eyes seem to be opened wider and as one of the witnesses is giving his testimony, he looks at him, sees and beholds the one who has been the cause of all the trouble. 

He leaves his father's office to walk home for dinner and his father asks him, "What do you think of the case?" 

The son says, "I looked, I saw; but what I saw, I cannot reveal. You had the guilty person in your office this morning and you should not try to prove him innocent. Someone has to suffer in this case, someone has to be given a sentence for it, do not fight to free the guilty and let the innocent suffer. 

If only I were a Lawyer today, I would gladly take the other side and what a case I could make of it. 

I am only a by-stander and must hold my peace yet awhile; but only for one more year. Wisdom, how wonderful! What great things it reveals! What a lesson; but I must keep this to myself until I can see it in its true light. 'Justice must prevail' shall be my motto."

After dinner, he meets his friend of yesterday and again they drive out to the golf links. This time no conversation is brought forth. His friend says, "I have nothing to say today, you gave me enough food for thought yesterday to keep me busy for a long time. How you think all these things out to see them the way you do is more than I can understand." "Today you may not; but tomorrow you may, if you want to. It is not by thinking, studying or

memorizing that I know these things, it is only by listening to the Voice of Creation; for only Creation can unravel the mysteries of the Creation." "There you go again, talking so deep that I would have to sink a well to get down to it and I never used tools of that kind in my life. Why do you not keep on the surface and give a man a chance?" "To read Creation, it is so easy it is hard." "What do you mean, so easy?" "All you have to do is see the principle and know how to use it and the principle of everything is easy, if you open your eyes." "Well, you told me you have never played golf; so I am going to test your theory today. I will teach you the principle of golf and then see how long it will take you to get it as it should be. I pride myself that I do understand golf.' "Yes, I know you are the champion and the pride of the golf world; but," smiling at him, he says, "if you want to keep your reputation, do not ask me to look into the principle." "Oh, yes, I will. I would like to have the experience of being beaten without having to play false.

I have mastered the principle of golf anyway." "Remember you ask for it; so as the saying goes, ‘ask for it and it shall be given unto you.' 999 Just before they start the game, his friend shows him the principle in the game of golf. He hits the first ball and it is a credit to him. Our young Mastermind then takes a ball, places it, gets in place and position in his own way not imitating his young friend in any way. The keen eye of the other sees this but says nothing. He just watches with interest. 

He has never seen a man look so natural in this

position as he does. He takes a glance in the direction of the play, lifts his club, then, before there was time to think, brings that club down with such force, that he sends the ball flyng through the air so fast that the young man who stands watching, watches the ball in amazement. His first ball has surpassed all records. Can this be possible? It is more than his companion can stand; so he says, "What a shot! I will bet you one hundred dollars you cannot repeat that stroke in the next ten balls.' 99 

Does our young man look at him with victory in his eyes? "No," but with a sympathy that can not be mistaken. "You must know the principle of everything. 

Would you play another ball for my satisfaction?" "This time I will try to do my best." He takes the ball, places it, stands over it and does not this time measure his field; but brings the club down with such force as only a mind or wisdom can. The ball fairly whistled through the air and goes much further than the first one. It looks almost impossible. He says, "You ask for it, and there it is. You are the only one who has seen it, I will never tell anyone else. No, I will not play today; but let us practice a little and enjoy the feel of knocking the ball so far that we have to walk to find it. That will give us an incentive to walk and will give us exercise because we want it and not because someone else told us it was good for us. It is what you want to do that counts-not what the other man plans that you should do." 

Once more our young Master-mind has been forced into action by the other man thinking he is

all-or has been made to believe by the world of make-believe.

On their way home, the young man says, "There is a fortune in golf for you." "Do you think so? I do not want to make a fortune in such a small way; and it would not be honest. I know the principle, the other man might think he does, yet he does not. So I would be taking unfair advantage over the other man and that would not be honest." "Well, anyway, we have a member here who thinks he has all the golf brains in the world. He talks of nothing else, he does nothng else. Would you take him on for a tournament, if he challenged you?" "I would not turn a challenge down; but I would not offer one." "You will have to wait until tomorrow." "Do not lose any sleep over it, and do not make a bet if this man challenges me. Who is he anyway ?" "He is Mr. So & So. He owns the biggest mill in town. He has nothing else to do but play the game and he surely walks over anyone who talks golf." "All right, for a few days I can afford to play and benefit by it, if I can come in contact with the best men in every game of life, that is what I am living for." "Meet me at 10:30 tomorrow morning so we will be at the Clubhouse when he arrives. 

At 10:30 the next morning, the young men are there talking golf in earnest, when the big man enters. He does not seem to notice them; but as he

stands a little apart from them, he hears one of the young men say, "Golf. The people in the city think they know the game. I met a young man yesterday who can play golf and I mean it."

The big man can stand the strain no longer. He walks toward the young men and asks, “Who is this great golf player you are talking of. I would like to meet him. He will have to prove it to me. I am the man of this club, when it comes to golf; and I am ready to challenge this young man you are talking about. No man can tell me the men of this city cannot play golf." "Then," says one of these young men, "You mean you will challenge this new found golf player?" "Yes Sir, and for any sum he may quote.' 99 Our young man sees a principle. This man has money to spare. He needs money to see him through college; so he accepts the challenge and lets the other man name the amount. The challenge is closed and the game is to start tomorrow.

The members of the club hear of this; but they all say, “Oh, well, someone will tell this young man who he is up against and he will be afraid to beat 

Mr. So & So." 

Will he? Let us wait and see.

They meet the next morning. There are many people there to see the game. They start, and at the first ball, everyone is amazed; for they have also heard that the young man has never played a game of golf before. Our young man keeps ahead of his opponent, just winning by a few. As the game ends, everyone sees the expression on the face of the big man of the city. He hands a check to the young man and says, "You won."

As they part another man comes up to him and says, "Do you know who you were playing? How dare you play to win?" 

He smiles and says, "Why?" "Did you not take his position into consideration?" "No, I never play the position, I play only the man. He might be greater than I in finance, but I am, it seems, his master at golf."

Our young man makes up his mind he will visit the golf links often for this would give him a glimpse into the principle of the bigger men as they are called. He plays, he sees, he reads, he understands and accumulates for future use. 

In the evenings he begins to take up medicinenot because he wants to be a Medical Doctor; but his Soul-mind is urging him into this path. Since it is his Soul-mind, he listens and obeys. 

The summer is over and once more, for his last term, he leaves for college with a wonderful anticipation for his future as a lawyer and a fulfillment of his parent's wish.

Once more he is a complete success and as he is on his way home at the end of the last year he is greeted by his lawyer friends in his own city. He has no difficulty in securing all necessary honors for his father stands high in the estimation of the high officials of the Law.

At last his father has his life long dream realized for on his sign he has painted, "Mr. and Son, Attorneys-at-Law."

He sits and listens to the clients as they come into his father's office. He has not missed one point of argument and is quick to understand; but says very

little for he is a silent listener. The first few days his father seems disappointed; but as his son starts to talk of the cases they have, he sees how far his son has gone into Law.

The people soon see in him an advanced Soulmind; and as he unravels the tangles of the Law, the big men begin to talk and their office is in more demand and he is kept busy day after day.

CHAPTER XI THE WHEEL 

As he walks home from the office he is met by the strange man once more. The greeting is mutual, each is so glad to see the other. He invites his 

Master-mind to his home for supper; but he says, "No, you see I eat so little and it may put your mother to extra work so I will eat at my hotel. Meet me on the hill at 7:30 tonight, I have very much to say to you." 99 

The Master-mind thinks when they part, "What a specimen of manhood; but the body is nothing compared to the mind that controls it. What a Soul-mind!" 

The young man, on his way home, thinks "How can I eat with such anticipation? I wish 7:30 were here now."

The time comes as all times come; and when the young man arrives he is greeted with such naturalness that he wonders how man can be natural in such an unnatural world. He, at once, knows and thinks, "Yes, I have met men in every stage of life; but this man stands alone. Man," he thinks, "but a man not of the world—a man alone from the world. I wonder if I will ever be as big as he?"

In these few moments, neither has spoken a word for each is thinking of the other's greatness. 

The strange man speaks thus: "Son of the Light, you are a credit to the world in which you live today;

THE WHEEL 

413 but some day you will be a light to the civilized world. Your life as a lawyer will bring you before the few; but your life of your real self will bring you before the world. I see you have started to master another field. I am proud of you. You are paying your debt honestly." "As I see it, it will take years to pay it; and to speak my mind truthfully, I never want to pay that debt. I want to go on paying it as long as this life lasts; then start again as the time goes on. I do want to be just what you would have me." "Son, there is where you make a mistake-not as great as I would have you but as great as you want to be; and with that wonderful Soul-mind there is no height you cannot reach. The people are telling and talking of the wonderful way you unravel the twists of the Law and how you show the principle of every Law. I read one of your talks on Law and could not help but cut it out of the paper. It was wonderful.” "What part of my speech was so wonderful that you thought it was worthwhile?" "I have it here and will let you read it by the light of this flash light I have with me." 

He reads thus: "No Law should be passed as 

Law that takes away the free will of the masses.

Only the free will of the individual should be controlled and that only when it is detrimental to the other person. A man who breaks a Law that injures himself only has paid the penalty already." "Now read the next thought slowly and carefully, go one." "If any individual or organization writes up a

Law to safe-guard the masses without the masses

asking for such a Law of protection; then that individual or organization has a hidden principle behind such Law for his own gain-not the protection of the masses as he would try to make them believe." "That was wonderfully and truthfully said; for common sense tells us that any Law that takes away the free will of the masses is a Law against the Law of Creation. The free will of the masses must not be tampered with. Why take the free will of the many to save the few who are not strong enough to use their free will; when we could protect the few without taking the free will, the Creator's greatest gift to man, from the many. This very thing has been the means of the fall of every great Nation. We, the massess, seek the line of least resistance, the few see our weakness and take possession of all, and can say to the masses, 'we will feed you or starve you as we wish. If you refuse to do our bidding, we will make laws to punish you. 

We are the masters, you are the slaves.' Did the 

Creator make things thus? No, man and man only can or would do these things. My Son of the Truth, talk the Law of Truth to the world, and soon we will have a New Era in this beautiful world in which we now live. The world in itself is beautiful; but man, it seems, is always trying to close the eyes of the other man for fear he sees too clearly the beauties of Creation. The few do not want the many to see; for if they did they would take steps to make the picture more evenly distributed. The store house of Creation was filled so every man should have his needs fulfilled. Why are they not more equally divided? It is the weakness of the few for power over his fellow-man-and this is

415 man alone. I did not meet you to talk of Law; but your future studies as a Medical Doctor. In this, as in your school of Law, look only for the principle. Be sure to keep an open mind. Do not reveal all the principles you see. You will need to bring these things forth in the future. Be observant and not talkative. You will find it fascinating yet disappointing; but keep right on with the principle. I have great things to reveal to you in the future. Suffering Humanity needs help and are constantly crying for it. Be ready for the Silent Voice. When it calls come forth and your greatest dreams you had as a boy will be fulfilled beyond your comprehension. "How can I ever pay you for the future you hold up to me; and tell me I am and will be big enough to reach a goal so high?" "Soon you will know how big you are not in your own estimation but in the estimation of the people. 

You see, a great man in the eyes of the world keeps wondering what kind of eyes those people must have when they can see so much greatness in a man who cannot see or realize any greatness in himself. You will find as you go along, the bigger you are in the eyes of the people, the more you realize how small you are compared to what you would want to be. 

The Path of Wisdom only can let man see himself in his true likeness; and as you gaze forward into the Path you see so much ahead of you that you want to know that it makes you feel as if you know nothing. Keep accumulating, there is no limit to the accumulating process. Never be satisfied with the

Wisdom you have, constantly be active in seeking more. Wisdom is as limitless as the Universe-and as sure as the Universe. Wisdom is the key to Na-

turalness and Naturalness is the Path to manhood and manhood is the stepping-stone to greatness, whether it be a Nation or an individual: For as the 

Leader of a Nation is so shall the Nation be. When all can see the true principle of the Leader and that principle is for his Nation's advancement; then the masses of the Nation should say, 'he is our Leader, we are his people.' "Sir, will the world ever see as you see, or know as you know?" "Yes, in time. You know time brings many changes; and there is the Dawn of a New Light coming forth, such as was never witnessed in the world before. Man will soon be taught the Truth of Creation and they will then see the Creator in all truth. The world will fall into this great truth, it will change the lives of men-and in turn, make all men men in the true sense of the word. When man is a man, he will not want to take from his fellowman what belongs to him by Creation. At the present time, it seems everyone is trying to take everything from the other person; but does not seem to care whether they can give anything in return. 

The password for the past few years has been, 'take as much as you can, but see that you give as little as possible.' What is life without the pleasure of giving, if that gift is being given without a thought of reward, either in the present or future?” "You surely understand the principle of life. 

May I call you my Master from now on? It seems so natural to use that phrase." "Yes, Son, you may; but time waits for no mannot even for the man of Wisdom. The night is get-

THE WHEEL 

417 ting late, so I will see you again soon. Again I say, 'good night, and I am proud of you.' 999 The young and the old part once more; but only in the flesh for their minds are full of thoughts of each other, one seeing the greatness of the one, the other seeing the greatness of the other to be. 

The next morning as he arises and dresses, he sees a picture that does not appear to him to be just. 

Clients who are coming to his office have had other lawyers before they came to him. In this picture he sees five men, each having a loaf of bread in his hand and a family to feed from this loaf. He sees them feed their children and sees that it takes all the loaf to feed them-not a crumb is left. As he gazes upon the mental picture, he thinks at least they seem to have enough. Then another man comes into view. He has a loaf too; but he is not satisfied with what he has. As he looks on the five feeding their loaf to their families, he thinks: "Since they have enough, why should I not take from them one-fifth of their loaf;" so he walks to each and takes his desired portion and the other five can say nothing, but submit no matter how unjust—this man has the rights of the Law to do this. He takes from them this portion and adds to his own. He can accumulate so why should he care how much he takes from these families as long as he gets his portion and is protected by the Law. The Silent 

Voice says, "There are enough Lawyers before you came on the scene. Every client who has left another Lawyer to come to you is taking a slice of his loaf. Can he afford to let you have it?" Just as he arrives at breakfast, he speaks his thoughts

aloud and they are these words: "I will look into this." 

At this his father says, "Just what are you going to look into, my Son?" "Dad, I have not time to go into what I mean; but you will see it illustrated today in my office."

As they reach the office they find the waiting room full of clients, both old and new; but they seem to all want the son or the young man as they call him. 

He sits down at his desk, lights his cigar then rings his bell. As each client appears before him, he asks them what he can do for them. Have they been to another lawyer? If they say, yes, then he asks why they desire to change. "Well, Sir, we have heard so much of your ability as a lawyer that we thought, why not have the best?" "Did your lawyer take your case?" "Yes." "Then go back to him."

If they answer that they have had no lawyer, he says, "Go back to the waiting room and I will call you when I am ready for you."

He soon clears the office for he has only two clients who have not had another lawyer. His father attends to his own people and can not understand why his son can get through with so many people in such a short time. Noon comes and as they go home for dinner, his father asks how he got through with so many clients in so short a time. "He smiles and says, "Dad, I just had two clients this morning." "Why, Son, your office was full of people." "Yes, but only two belonged to me. All the others

THE WHEEL 

419 had lawyers already; but wanted to turn their cases over to me. I will never eat the bread from another man's loaf unless I am sure that he has enough and to spare." "Son, do you mean to tell me that you turned all that business away?" "Yes, Dad, it did not belong to me. They came for the name and the fame they think I have. I will only take on cases that have not another lawyer. 

You see, I am not going to be a lawyer for long; then why get tied up in it in knots that I may not be able to untie when I want to. I took up Law to please mother and you. I am now taking up medicine to please myself—50-50. Fair, is it not? Why take them from the other man today then throw them back tomorrow? I will not be in the office this winter. I have to go to the School of Medicine for three years, whether I need to or not. That is the

Law-go to school so long, pay so much money and they will give you what they think. That is Law, whether it be a just Law or not. It is not the man who makes the doctor, it is the Law." "Son, be careful how you express yourself. All I can say is 'would that I were a man as big as you.' 

You change everything around. Most sons would like to be as their father, your father would like to be as his son; but he feels he has to follow the material and the unnatural to be anything else but what he is." "Never mind, Dad, you have not many years to live in this life, and when you come back the world will be further advanced towards the truth of life; then you will fall in line. What is one life in this thing we call 'Time?' Be not discouraged, you will

get there as you travel the Road of Time. You know time has no end for the mind or I should say Soul-mind. That part of us is Creation, the material part just comes and goes to the soil." "All right, Son, as you say, I believe. Everything sounds reasonable and the highest common sense; but it is so new to us that we do not grasp it and see it as you do. It is time we are getting back to the office again. Once more, I say, I am mystified, I do not understand you; but I am proud to be your father."

At these words the mother speaks for the first time during the meal and her words are: "Such a boy!" "Yes, and such a mother that boy has," as he puts his arms around her to take his midday caress. "Home, yes, home," he whispers to himself as he and his father leave for the office.

Days pass by. He is kept busy; but does not take a client from any of the other lawyers. He is always successful with every case he takes in hand.

He decides if he has to study medicine he will have to spend less time in his office of Law; so he begins to close his doors to clients except for a few hours a day. This gives him more time to see into the principle of this new path of his he has started. He does not mix but is in constant demand as an authority of the Law. Almost all of the lawyers feel his superiority. Some resent this; yet others speak well of him. He cares not what they have to say, he is immune to the opinion of others. There are only two persons he heeds-they are his Master-mind and the one he loves-not for his seemingly greatness, but for himself alone. The Master puts him

THE WHEEL 

421 on the Path of Wisdom and as he looks ahead he can always see the one he loves ahead of him and always with a smile of love which seems to say, "Yes, I am waiting, always waiting, come.' 19 He thinks, "Two ideals, one to be big enough to reach the woman he loved, and the other to reach the greatest height of manhood." 

Will he reach these two ideals? Let us watch him step by step-this once little piece of a Human 

Rose; but so misunderstood. 

The days fly quickly. The time for Medical

School is here. He leaves his home, his office and his loved one; but has he any regret? No, he is following the Path the Silent Voice of his Soul-mind has laid bare for him.

Once more he enters the routine of college; but he has the advantage over other students. He is here because he wants to, not because he has to; so instead of a drudgery it is a pleasure. He enters the work with all his power of Wisdom, and as he goes through, he keeps silent. He only observes; and answers questions when they are put to him. He asks the great men questions only when he is alone with them, and as he does, his questions startle them.

He forces the answer from them by the natural way he has. He soon becomes a mystery to them; yet pulls them up if they say anything which he cannot see clearly. They must explain for that is what he is there for. He tells them he wants to know all that they know so that he can measure the capacity of the field of their knowledge. 

He is listening to a lecture and the Professor uses the words, "It may be this or it may come from that or it may be something else." He can restrain him-

self no longer so at the first opportunity he asks if the Medical Professor can not say definitely what disease comes from? If they can not, where is the knowledge they are supposed to have of the cause of the diseases that they might be called upon to diagnose? Is not sickness the victim of a cause, and is not a Doctor supposed to be able to see the cause? If he can not understand and actually know the cause, how can they get the effect or give the right thing to effect a cure? How can they effect a cure, if they are not positive of the cause?" 

At this question, the Professor says, "Young man, you will find the human body is subject to so many diseases that it is hard to actually find the cause in every case." "Then if there is, why give medicine when you do not know whether it would do good or harm?"

At this time, the session was over, but the perspiration is seen on the Professor's forehead. As he leaves the room, he says, "What a brain that young man has! I will have to talk to him in my office. He must not ask these questions in the presence of the other students. An advanced mind, I would say."

Our young man is invited to meet the Professor that night. From then on he restrains himself in public, but not in private. All through the term he keeps a quiet reserve that pleases the staff of the school. He becomes a constant visitor to the head of the organization. Collecting? Yes. Memorizing? No; but seeing never to forget anything he sees.

The term comes to a close and on his way home

THE WHEEL 

423 he thinks and sees, as he says, "Food for my future life."

When he arrives home he is greeted by his parents; but that is not the biggest greeting. The love on the lone hill whom he knows will be waiting for him fills him with a joy that words can not express.

After supper he starts up the hill with such anticipation as he has never experienced before. As he nears the spot, he sees her sitting there gazing, not into space, but trying to penetrate the darkness to see the one person who meant all to her. She silently whispers, "Will I ever be worthy of him? 

If ever a woman was worthy of any man, I will surely try to be that woman." 

His silent thoughts are: "Yes, woman can make or mar, just as she pleases. Woman controls the world. Man may think he does, but does he? Do you control your world?" He smiles as he thinks how wonderful she is and says, "No, I would not want to." 

As they meet and greet, neither can say much. 

He only says, "So you are here," and she smiles and says, "and you came."

They sit together until late in the night; but their conversation we will leave as sacred for this time. 

They walk home. I can just imagine the smile of the moon as it whispers, "I am an eye witness; but 

I am a silent watcher. What I hear, I forget; what 

I never tell. I am a true friend to love." 

I see,

The next morning he goes to his office for business once more. The people begin to come and he is soon busy straightening out the tangles of the 

Law. His life is a busy one,

CHAPTER XII  

A few weeks after he is home, they are at the breakfast table when they hear the paper boy crying his head-line of a dastardly crime committed in a small village. They get a paper, and as they read, each passes his opinion. The crime is the talk of the city. The papers state they have found the murderer some distance from the spot where they have found the body. Everyone seems to be satisfied that they have the right man. Everything proves it; so this young man is held for the crime. Day after day the papers make more of it and the people are incited against the young man almost to the point where the masses cry for his life.

As our young Master-mind reads the paper, he sees some loop-holes for doubt and this makes him take more than usual interest in the case. The date for the trial is set and is looked forward to, by the people with interest. 

One day as our young master is walking home, he is met by his Master-mind once more. As they stop his Master-mind says, "I want you to meet me on the hill tonight at eight-thirty. I have much to say to you. I will not delay you; but will expect to see you later." 

Eight-thirty comes and they meet. As they sit down, the Master-mind begins by saying, "Can you remember the words I said to you some years ago?

These were the words: 'I will need a great lawyer some day.' Well, that day has come. When I read of this crime, I saw a loop-hole for doubt; so I called on the mother of the young man. I heard her story. I went to see the young man and heard his story after I persuaded him to talk. They are poor and need a lawyer; so that is why I am here to see you tonight. I told the mother I knew of a lawyer who could and would defend her son. I want you to take this case and as you go through the evidence, listen to the Silent Voice and you can make no mistake. As the voice speaks so shall thou speak. Hold not back. The trial is in a few days for they have fear of the people; so go tomorrow, offer your services for the defense. First go to the mother and get her story, then visit the boy in prison. Then have an interview with the constable who found the child. 

What you will see, you will see." "Yes Sir, I know what you mean for I have seen already." "So your eyes have been opened. It is well. Once more, I say, keep these words before thee always:

Seek Wisdom and all things will be revealed unto thee. What words they are and how true. I hear someone else coming so I will leave you. I put my trust in thee to see justice through, just as my Creator put his trust in me." "My Master, I will not betray your trust." "I am proud of you, my young Master-mind. 

Good night."

As he leaves, someone else takes his place, just as great only in a different way. They are soon in conversation of things that are nearest their hearts; yet he seems to her to be in two places at one time

for she has to repeat things so many times. At last she asks, “Do you have something on your mind that means more to you than the things I am saying to you?" "Yes, a mother's love and a son's imprisonment." "Oh, the crime that was committed in the village?" "Yes, I am going tomorrow to take up the defense of the young man." "According to the papers, there is no doubt that he is guilty of the crime," she says; "but since you are going to take the side of defense, you must see further than anyone else for I once heard you say, 'As long as I am a Lawyer I will never try to prove the guilty innocent nor the innocent guilty.' That tells me, if you are going to take the defense, you must see what no other man can see." "Yes, one other man. I understand, I am satisfied." "I will watch the case with interest." 

The next day, he tells his father he is not going to the office. He is going to the scene of the murder. The father asks, "Why are you going there?" "I am going to take up the defense.' 99 "Son, do you realize what you are doing? That young man has been proven, without doubt, the guilty man." "Yes, Father, I realize everything; but all the evidence is circumstantial." "Yes, but a proven fact. You had better read the case again as it was put before the public in the papers; then maybe you will change your mind.” "No, Father, nothing can change my mind." "I know for I remember the case over a year

ago when you told me I had the guilty party in my office and your words came true; yet that day I did not believe you. You are your own master, so 

I have nothing to say. If I had, I would say, 'hands off that case; but as you will.' You must see as no other man sees. Remember your words: 'I will not take a case unless the principle is clean.'"

He smiles at these words and leaves the breakfast table. "I will see you tonight. If there are any clients for me today, tell them to come back tomorrow."

When he reaches the village, the scene of the crime, he goes first to the mother of the young man and she tells him her story. (Her story will be told at the trial so we will go with him as he goes to the prison head-quarters.) He sends his card in and is admitted to the officials of the prison. He tells them he is going to be the defending lawyer on the murder case. 

They say, "What are you going to defend? 

Everything points to the prisoner's guilt without a doubt." "I would like to see my client, the prisoner." "All right, come this way."

He is left in the cell with the young man. For a few moments, he does not speak. The young man is hanging his head and will not look at him; so he addresses him thus: "Young man, lift up your head, I want to talk to you and I want you to talk to me. 

I am your lawyer and I am going to see what I can do for you; so I want you to tell me your story of the day of the crime." 

The young man lifts his head and says, "Sir, do not make it any harder for me than it is. I have

been drilled by one, and then another so much that 

I am ready to say, 'yes, I did it' whether I did or not. Such drilling, such questioning, such cornering. I never thought there could be so much cruelty in the world-and they call it Law.-Law nothing, they are the Fiends of Hell. Am I not punished enough to be found guilty of a crime of which I can remember nothing, without this cruel cross-questioning going on constantly?" "That is enough of your raving, young man. Let us get down to business. I am here as a friend, not an enemy; so calm yourself as much as you can. 

Look at me, tell me everything that you know of the doings of that day. Do not leave one part of that day out." "Sir, you make it easy. Everyone else I have seen has been trying to make me confess to things I know nothing about. I will give you a true story of that day; but I will have to go back to the beginning and that was the Saturday before the day of the crime. 

I had a girl whom I loved more than life. We were going to be married someday; but my mother being a widow, I could not leave her yet awhile. My girl said she was willing to wait. Last Saturday night when I went to meet her, I stood waiting for her, I saw her with another young man, a stranger who had just come to the village. I went to her, expecting she would leave him and come to me; but she smiled and said, 'I will see you tomorrow.' I felt, well, if you have ever been in love and saw the woman you love pass you by with another man, then you can understand, otherwise, this story may mean nothing to you."

"I came to listen to you. Do not leave anything out. A little word may mean so much."

The young man becomes interested and tells his story how that week he could neither eat nor sleep. 

His mind tortured him day after day, night after night; then pay-day came and he took part of it to his mother and made up his mind he would go and get drunk. He had never tasted drink in his life; but he went to the hotel. He was excited and nervous. He drank until he felt himself getting dizzy; then he thought he would take a bottle home and maybe he could go home and sleep-and forget.

After he left the hotel, he felt he was losing the use of his legs and he felt a strange feeling in his head. He remembered laying down in the field he had to cross on his way home. That was the last he knew until he woke and found himself in jail charged with committing the crime. "Did you know the girl?" "Yes Sir, she was the life of the village. Everyone knew her for she was the village pet, so sweet and always wore a smile." "All during this conversation our young Mastermind has been busy; but he looks and sees no mental picture. As he leaves he holds out his hand to shake the hand that is supposed to have shed blood. 

The young man draws back as he says, "Do you want to shake the hand that shed the blood of a pure young girl?"

Our Master-mind takes his hand in spite of the hesitation on the part of the other and says, "I will take a chance," as he says "good-bye; but remember, eat and be silent until I see you again."

When he comes in the office, they ask him what he got out of the prisoner. "Wait until the day of the trial," he says, and leaves them. He goes to the home of the constable and is invited in. "Constable, I am on the defense side of the criminal murder case and I would like your story. You surely did your work well. Would you mind telling me about it?” "No Sir, I am willing at any time to relate all I know of the case." "I would like first if you would tell me as much of the young man's life as you can." "All right. This young man, as far as I know, has always been clean and well respected. I do not think he ever took a drink in his life before that day. When I saw him leave the hotel, I was surprised to see him under the influence of drink; but every young man, it seems, comes to the time when he feels he should try it once. I saw him start for home. Not long after I saw our little girl of the village come to a store nearby where I was standing. 

She always had a smile and a word for me. I saw her leave the store, it was then beginning to get dark. I told her she better hurry home before it got dark. She said, 'I am not afraid.' About two hours after, her parents came to me and asked if I had seen their little girl. I told them, yes, and the time

I saw her on her way home. They told me she never arrived home and would I help find her. We got some men together and the search began, one going one way, another another way. We could not find her so I suggested that we go to the old brickyard. We went and there two young men found

her partly covered with loose brick. A search was then started for the one who had committed the crime. We searched all around but could find no clue. The ground was hard and there was not a footmark to be found. The search went on and the searchers began to increase as the news spread around the village. I suggested that anyone who had committed the crime would naturally leave the village as soon as possible; so they took their way in every direction. We soon heard a noise and some man calling. When I got to them we found young lying drunk and was blood from head to foot. He had drunk the contents of the bottle and did not know anything. As we took him to the jail, the people wanted to string him up right there; but I got them to listen to me and got him safely to the jail. What a job that was. I will never forget the rage of the mob. Next morning he was brought before the Judge and charged with murder in the first degree. He did not understand, he just seemed to be in a daze. Sir, she was the sweetest girl in the village and a pal to everyone." At these words the constable wipes a tear from his eye and sits and gazes into space. 

Our young Lawyer does not speak all this time; but as he gets up to leave, he says, "Yes, everything points to the guilt of this young man. Do you think he is guilty, Constable?" "Yes Sir, who else could be." "Oh well," says our young master, "after all,

Constable, it is only circumstantial evidence; but everyone seems to take it for granted that he did. commit the crime. As time goes on we will see; then we will know what we do know. It is possible, Con-

stable, that he may not be guilty after all" and he smiles as he leaves the home of the constable. It is not a smile of mirth, but a smile as the words came to him-"Seek Wisdom and all things shall be revealed unto you."

As he travels home, he has these thoughts: 

The world has condemned the young man already. 

Would he be big enough to save him? Then the words come again through that beautiful mind, "The innocent must not suffer for the guilty." He sees, he understands. When he reaches home for supper his father is waiting anxiously for him to ask him questions on the case. He starts to question him, but his son says, "A good lawyer never talks of his case to others. One can keep a secret, but two, never; so, Father, do not mention the case again. I will say this, I am going to defend this young man's life with all the power of speech I can bring to bear on the day of the trial. Until that day, I am silent."

Days come and go, but the trial of this young man never is forgotten.
