Each day it is the discussion, yet not a discussion, just an agreeing that he is guilty and what they should do with a man who would commit such a crime-not only the village but all the surrounding towns are anxious for the day of the trial. At last the day comes. The courtroom is filled to capacity. The street is lined with hundreds of people. This is the only trial on the books for that day and what a trial it is going to be. As the Jury files into their seats, you can see stamped on their faces just what their verdict is going to be. As the Judge takes his place, he, too, has a stern and solemn look on his

face. You can see anxiety in his face for he has before him in court a mob such as he has never witnessed before in all his judgeship. As he gives orders for the prisoner to be brought into the court, the crowd sways with excitement. The police are stationed everywhere in the courtroom. Everything has been prepared for the worst. As they lead the prisoner in, the hissing and storming is fierce. The 

Judge demands quiet in the court; but it takes a considerable time to get quietness so they can proceed with the trial. When the case is opened, the constable is called to give his picture of the case. 

As he does so, our young lawyer looks steadily toward him. Before he finishes, he seems to collapse. 

He says she was the sweetest girl and the scene was too much for him. 

The Judge says, "That will do. Call the mother of this young man.' 99 She comes forward and our young lawyer takes her by the arm and leads her to the witness stand. 

The Judge addresses her thus: "Tell us all about your boy and the time he left home on the day of the murder."

She tells the story of her boy's life and his disappointment in love and the days of torture he went through. "So you said your boy never took drink before that day. All right, sit down. Call the men who found the body." 

They gave their testimony; then he says, "Call the men who found the prisoner." They come and tell how they found him drunk, stained with blood and with an empty bottle in his hand, and that he was so drunk they could not do anything with him.

"Call the Constable again." He is again on the witness stand. "You are the Constable in this village. 

What was the character of the young man previous to this day?"

He speaks well of him and tells how he was surprised to see him leave the hotel under the influence of drink. "What time did you see him leave the hotel, and what time did you see this girl go and come from the store?" "As near as I could say, about fifteen minutes." "Would this young man meet the girl as he went on his way home when she came to the store?" "Yes Sir, they would use the same footpath through the field as we call it." "All right, that will do. The Prosecuting Attorney will now take the stand and state his side of the case for the benefit of the Law; but remember you are talking in the interest of the prosecution, only to try to do justice to the victim and the living.' 

As the Prosecuting Attorney rises, the Judge has to cry for silence in the Court, the commotion is so great. At last silence prevails and the prosecutor starts by speaking to the court thus: "Your Honor, the Judge and Jury, it is my duty for the Law to bring before you a picture of a most dastardly crime committed in our very presence; and to bring the criminal to justice. I have studied this case from every angle and I find this boy's life has been anything but good. They tell the story of a disappointed love; but I see more than this. I see a young man who appears before the people in the village with a smile of innocence. It is my duty to look below the surface and I have found that he

often met this girl and walked home with her. I can see the working of an evil mind, constantly contemplating and anticipating the very thing that happened. I can also see that he had not the courage to carry out his intentions in his sober senses; so he watched and waited until he could have an excuse for getting intoxicated. This all proves to me the low, degraded undercurrent that was hidden behind his smile of deceit. The time came and he got drunk, carried another bottle with him to help his cowardly courage so he might carry this thing through. He, I will admit, did not expect the results in this most unmanly deed; but when he saw the damage, he flew into a rage and finished the most gruesome deed that any man can conjure in his mind. It is easy to see the whole thing as we look at it from beginning to end. An evil mind has no conscience. Men like these are not fit for the name of man. It is clear evidence that this was planned days before it happened. I ask you, the

Jury, to have no mercy on the prisoner. He had no mercy on this dear little girl who we are told was the flower of the village. Yes, a beautiful flower cut off in the most dastardly way that could be conceived by the mind of a fiend—not a man. Justice calls for a life for a life. I would say, have mercy on him even as he had mercy on his victim. There is, I know, no doubt in the minds of the people that he is guilty. So you," looking at the Jury, "are here to see that justice prevails. Do not fail to do your duty. I am done." 

The Judge: "Do you want want to question the prisoner ?" "No, your Honor, it is not necessary."

As he sits down, the people in Court almost burst into cheers; but the Judge calls for silence. 

He calls the Attorney for the defense.

As our young Master-mind rises, a quiet feeling goes through the Court. The people see standing before them a beautiful specimen of manhood, a face that shows no fear, and eyes beaming with intelligence as he looks at the court-with what they think is a smile. A smile?—No, only the natural expression of a natural man. As he turns his face away from the people to face the Judge, his clear steady voice says, "Your Honor, the

Judge;" then turning slowly but gazing steadily at the Jury, “and the Jury, you have heard the testimony of the witnesses of this case, also your able Prosecuting Attorney. As I listened to him, I just wondered how any man could bring up such convincing facts as he brought before you. It almost seems to me that a man who could tell about all the degrading pictures of this young man's life would have to have had similar experiences in actual life, himself, before he could paint a picture so clear; but also I have a picture which I will put before you. I will admit that the evidence against the prisoner is strong and everything points to his guilt; yet no one saw him commit the crime. Oh, I know what your thoughts are. 'Did he not have proof of his guilt by his clothes?' I say, No, he did not. 

The Prosecuting Attorney said justice must prevail.

I also say justice must prevail; but in this case a life is the forfeit so we must reason this thing out. Decide in haste, repent at leisure. Now let me paint a picture as I see this tragedy. You heard the mother's story, so did I. You heard the witnesses,

so did I. We will not go over it again. I know it is still fresh in your minds; but that is just one picture. I will put another picture beside it and I will leave you to compare. Now this young man, through the sorrow of his heart of an unfaithful lover, became lovesick. He could not eat, sleep or work. You may call this weakness, but I say it is manliness. If any of you have been in love and this has happened to you then you will understand this young man's feelings. He stood it until the day of the crime and on that day he sought, in drink, to drown his sadness. He drank too much then he bought a bottle to take home that he might sleep and forget his sorrow. Leaving the hotel he was seen by the Constable who was surprised to see him in this state; but he saw him start for home. Soon the young man was too drunk to go further; so he laid down which is natural for anyone to do through the influence of drink. In the meantime the girl was seen going to and from the store by the Constable. 

It was then beginning to get dark. That is all we know until she was found murdered some two hours after. Now, here is where a new picture comes in. 

Someone came upon the girl. He lost his balance and his manhood, put his arms around this little girl. She was going to scream and he placed his hand upon her mouth and held it there until he had committed an outrage. Then he saw what he had done, therefore, he finished his unmanly work by covering up the body. He then walked away in haste and had not gone far when he stumbled over something. He felt and found it was someone lying drunk. A frenzied mind works quickly; so he saw a loop-hole out of the position in which he found

himself. He carried this drunken man to the side of his victim, smeared him with blood; then carried him away again from the scene of the murder and laid him down and found the bottle in the man's pocket, poured the contents into his second victim then hastened away from the scene. This could happen. 

So you see there is a great loop-hole in the evidence of a few moments ago. Your Honor, the Judge and the Jury, I am not here to plead for this young man's life, I am here to save this crime from being the means of further crime. I am not here to plead for mercy for the prisoner, I am hear to prevent a more dastardly crime from being performed. 

At present there is just one victim and one murderer; but if you find this young man guilty and take his life, it will not only make one murderer, it will make fifteen murderers and two victims. If you find him guilty and he pays the penalty and someone confesses to the murder, that will put the blood of this young man upon all your hands as long as you live and a seething fire in your conscience to carry to your last day. I would save you from this, I do not ask for his liberty at once; but I do say the evidence is not strong enough to condemn the young man, therefore, I ask that his case be adjourned until the next criminal session, six months hence. Again, I say, I am not pleading for this man's life-only justice, and to save the conscience of the many-that is all. I thank you." As he says this, he again turns to the people in the Courtroom and with his natural smile says, "Again, I thank you." 

The Judge then speaks to the Jury, "You have heard the Prosecution, you have heard the defense

and the request for an adjournment. As is your verdict so shall it be."

The Jury files into the jury-room. 

What will be their verdict?

As the Jury file into their room, a change comes over the people in the courtroom. There is no commotion, but as they leave their seats they seem to form circles and their attitude has changed towards the prisoner. You can see shame in their faces and a doubt. The people who, a few hours ago, condemned this young man now are wavering. 

They wonder who this young lawyer is who can change their thoughts; but as they express themselves, "What a picture he gives! What a wonderful voice he has! You would think that he was seeing the crime committed, so clear is the picture he paints. You know," some are heard to say, "a

Prosecuting Attorney is not a very high position and we know he did not tell the truth about the young man's life. If he did do it, it was on the spur of the moment through drink." Another says, "I really do not think he did commit the crime;" so the conversation goes on in all parts of the Courtroom, a few moments a condemning crowd, now a doubtful crowd. How long will they be in doubt? No one knows how long the Jury will be out, but no one leaves the Court. The issue, since there are two sides, has become interesting. 

Soon there seems to be some commotion; they are bringing the prisoner back into Court. The lawyers are taking their places. When they lead the prisoner in this time there is a quiet rustling of feet. No one wants to make a noise. They do not show any indignation as before. There is a

silent power, a mysterious feeling in the atmosphere of the Court. Everyone is expecting something but they do not know what.

The Jury files in after being out only thirty minutes. The silent thought in the mind of everyone is: "What will the verdict be?" 

The foreman of the Jury steps up to the Judge. 

You can see the Judge's face change from the stern face of a man who has his mind made up to a man who is not sure of himself. He slowly opens the paper, reads the verdict to himself and as he does, you can see relief come over all the features of his face. 

The verdict is "Not Guilty." As he pronounces the words aloud the courthouse seems to heave a great sigh; but there is not a word of dissent from the house. The mother goes to her son and the feeling in Court is a sight to behold. 

The Judge then says, "Clear the Court, the trial is over.' 99 Our young Master-mind goes over to the young man and takes his hand and smiles as he says, "I took a chance, did I not?" 

The young prisoner looks at him with tears in his eyes. He is so full he can only say, "Words are not big enough."

The next day the young lawyer has a piece in the paper directly underneath the printing of the trial: "Anyone who tries to insult this young man, no matter what he is, will have to pay and pay dearly for it."

The young man goes home. As the people read the speech of the Defending lawyer, they are satisfied with the verdict but they all come to one con-

clusion: "This young lawyer could see and did know more of the crime than anyone else; so it will be up to the Law to find the right murderer." They say, "I wonder who the murderer could be?"

This case, like other things in life, is becoming a memory to the people. Only the parents are still in their silent tears for their loved little daughter. 

Time passes on and our Master-mind is congratulated by all who know him; but yet there is doubt in many of their minds.

Six months pass and the murderer has not been found or they have found no clue. 

Our young lawyer is off to school of Medicine for another term. We will leave him alone this term as we know he is there to find the principle only of the World of Medicine and Research. We will meet him at his home the first morning after the school term is over:

His father is at the breakfast table, as his son walks into the breakfast-room. Each looks at the other and says "Good Morning." His father says, "I sat up late last night to meet you; but I could not hold out so I went to bed. I heard you come in and heard the clock strike every hour. I thought morning would never come. You will have a lot of work today, there are so many waiting for you. 

Son, I am proud yet jealous of you." "Have you heard anything of that murder, Father? Have they not found the man? It makes me smile when I think how slow they are; but it will out sooner or later." "Son, I sometimes think you made a mistake in that case." "How could I make a mistake? They had noth-

ing to stand on. Even if I had the case all over again, I could bring stronger evidence of his innocence.' 99 "You could?" "Yes, you see, I work by the principle and if the principle is true, it leaves no room for doubt." At this moment, the telegraph boy comes to the door.

The young lawyer goes to the door. The telegram reads: "Come at once to the Constable's home. 

Come as quickly as possible." He reads the telegram again and shows it to his father and says, "I will have a story worthwhile the telling when I meet you again." 

He does not take heed of his speed. As he goes into the house, he is met by the minister and the Doctor. He goes upstairs to the bed-room and as soon as the Constable sees him, he says, "Now I am ready." He then confesses the murder of the little girl, telling everything. After he tells all, he says, “They will not get me. I am going."

The Doctor goes to his side; but he gives one deep breath of relief then slips from the hands of the Law.

The news is spread and the confession published in the papers. Can you imagine fifteen special persons who read this and what they feel toward this young lawyer who it seems controlled all their minds at one time to save them from the picture he put before them? When the Judge reads the confession, he goes to his desk and opens a sealed letter and compares the contents with the confession. All he can say is, "A mystery, yes, too much for me: but those eyes, that voice and what an artist! A lawyer? No, a masterpiece in naturalness.' 99

How true these words are. This again brings our young Master-mind before the public as a man alone in the world of Law; but how he can be able to know the guilty and the innocent in this case is more than they can understand. Only a Mastermind can see the mental picture of another man's mind. 

Time speeds on. His life goes on, mastering every principle in everything, but holding himself down to his natural self. Success means nothing to him. He does not take credit unto himself, if he can possibly put it on another. Only two persons see and understand. He says, "I have just two things in life worthwhile. The one is to be big enough to be worthy of the one I love, and the other is to live to pay my debt to my Master-mind." 

Once more he is the talk of the world in which he lives; but months pass and the day comes when he is preparing for his last year at the school of medicine. His last day as a lawyer comes. As he meets his father and mother at breakfast, they look sad. He gazes upon them, smiles and says, "This is my last day as a lawyer. I want you to take my name off the plate on the office door. I fulfilled your request, now I am going out for myself, and to me, my biggest achievement of all is the relieving of Suffering Humanity. What a field for labor! 

What a chance to do something worthwhile! Life is too short to live to fulfill only one mission. I am going to fulfill many." "Are you going to specialize on any one disease?" "If I did that would be an insult to my Creator.

He gave me a mind without limit. There are hundreds of diseases of which the body is a victim.

Surely, if I have a mind without limit and I condense that limitless mind down to one disease, what will my Creator think of me? No, let the other man do this, it is too small for me. I will see the principle of every disease and unless I know the cause, I will never try to effect a cure in my life. As a Doctor, I will know and do my duty or I will not know and will do nothing." "I see," his father says smiling, "you are still going to live and do your duty through this great principle; but it is too deep for me. Still I am proud to be your father." "And I am the mother of a wonderful son. Someone is going to have a wonderful husband. If ever you meet the right girl I would like to meet her." "Then, Mother, you will meet her tonight." "I will be glad. She must be a girl worthwhile, if you have chosen her and your choice is my choice. 

I will be glad to meet my future daughter.' 

As they leave for the office, his father says, "Well, this is your last day; but in these three years you have made a name that will go down in the history of the Lawyers' world. I know you will be more successful in your life as a Doctor for that was your heart's desire when a boy. So man can be what he wants if he wants to hard enough?" "Yes, if he keeps money out of it, but if money is the motive power then it keeps you in the wheel of industry-that makes man just a cog after all. I refused to be a cog wheel in the Law and I will not be a cog in the wheel of the Medical World, I want to find out all about medicine, what it will do, what it will not do, what per cent of the diseases it can reach and what percentage it really cannot reach;

then I will observe other methods of healing and watch them also. I will keep an open mind, I will not bear prejudice in any line of healing. What meets the cause, that I will use. I am not in life for either money or fame, but how much I can do for my fellow-man." "Son, you surely have proved that statement to be true in the last few years.' 99 By this time they have arrived at the office. He meets his clients, asks them if they want to turn over their cases to his father or would they rather go to someone else, as this is his last day in his office. As the day goes on, he has his books of Law put into boxes, nails them down and says with a smile, "Good bye Old Friends, we met, we part. I hope I served you well. I know I did not soil nor use you roughly." He then turns to his office girl and says, "Did you get another office yet?" "Yes, Sir, I did." "That is all right. I am pleased to know that."

Soon the time to close comes. He moves out without a regret for he is going out not because he has to but because he wants to. When he reaches home that night he says, "Well, a lawyer no more, how glad I am. Law," then he smiles a smile only his father understands.

CHAPTER XIII  

That night he brings his future wife to meet her future mother. As they meet he watches them with his eyes of wisdom so he can see the mental picture in their eyes. He smiles and thinks, "Yes, they will intermingle or blend smoothly. I am satisfied."

They spend a pleasant evening together and peace reigns supreme. Each is satisfied with the other. 

The mother says, "Son, you choose well, and my daughter to be, you choose well. I can see a happy blending." "No, Mother, only the circle complete once more.

This blending has been going on for years and years." "Son, I hope your circle you speak of may never be broken. I am afraid there are few circles but what need welding in the world today. It must be wonderful to be understood."

As the night comes to a close, he takes her home and this time does not leave here at the door, he follows her in and meets her parents and stays long enough to become loved by his future mother-in-law and respected by his future father-in-law; then he leaves saying, "Someday, I will take your daughter, but I will give you a son."

The next morning, he leaves for his last year in the School of Medicine. This is the most interesting of all. He thinks, I will not only know what all the world of Medicine and Surgery know, but I will

know what they think, and how they come to the knowledge of research. I will know just what they know, everything they can do and what they cannot do. I wonder what the percentage will be for and against man's mind. How great, yet how few know it. Well, I am going to go the limit in the Medical 

World. I wonder how big it is as it stands today?"

As the train pulls into a station, he is surprised to see his Master-mind get on the train. He rushes to the entrance to meet him for this is the first time since before his master stroke in his life of Law. 

As they sit together, he knows his Master-mind had met him for a purpose; so he sits silent until his master speaks. "So this is your last year? Did you give up your office of Law?" "Yes Sir." "It is well. My purpose for this meeting is to talk of the outcome of your last year of school. 

The first half, keep your eyes open and your ears keen. Do not ask too many questions or you may gain disapproval. This is not losing yourself but showing your bigness by seeing and hearing things that will disgust you, and, being big enough to go through it, you know you will be as I am, one man against the many or, I should say, one man whom the many are against. The more you keep quiet, the more they will reveal their true selves unto you. 

When the time comes when they ask you to become one of them, do as they do, know as they know, see as they see; then will be your time to refuse to give up one particle of your free will-not even if they should threaten to keep you back. If this happens, tell them what you know, what they do

not know and let them see how you stand in life.

Many know you now and that you live by the principle." "I thank you for meeting me and telling me these things. Now I know just what you see that I should want to do. I will not betray my trust." "Then I will leave at the next station, and as I go I say, 'fear no man, no combination of menfor Creation is master of them all.' Good-bye, once more." 66 As he leaves his young Master-mind, he says, ‘Again, what a body, but what a mind.” 

The young man thinks "Remember, whenever you need me I will not fail you."

By this time his train pulls into the station. On the platform he is greeted by his former collegians who all have a word for him; so he again enters the world of routine, but not to him for he knows no routine.

The season goes on and he keeps busy seeing, hearing, observing, anticipating and looking into the principle from every angle so he might not miss one point in the field of the Medical World. Will he be satisfied with the knowledge or Education he receives? Will it hold water? We will see as the season closes.

He is received at the school by all with great interest, for had not his name become famous as a 

Lawyer? The one question is: "Why does he want to give up Law and take up medicine?" As he meets the officials of the college, one of them says, "You surely made a wonderful case out of that murder. 

How did you know the young man was not guilty?" "Oh, well, you see, I am a man of Principle. I

work by this principle, and the principle must be clean before I will use it. I am not of the masses, I am a looker-on. I refuse to be an imitator in any line of life. I claim that if any man finds a principle, so can I. If anyone can know anything, so can 

I. As an illustration, in your world of medicine and surgery, you follow the line that has been laid out for you by someone else, and by Law these methods are the standards of efficiency. The best trained imitator gets the highest mark. He who can imitate this man's methods, in the eyes of what he calls his superiors, gets the most credit for his work. As I go through this routine, I do not take one line from the other man in earnest. I enter into the game not for what I get out of it but just to feel how small a man can be, and how easy he is led to believe he knows something or does something when in reality he knows nothing, neither can he do anything. It is just imitation. As I go along I say to myself and I mean it, no matter who the man might be or what he can do in any line of medicine or surgery, so can I.-Not in his way but in my own original way. My brain is as good as his and my mind is as great as his. If his way of doing falls in line with mine and 

I cannot improve upon it, then I do the same as henot because I 'have to' but because I 'want to.'

There are hundreds of things I have seen you have taught that I would never think of imitating. The principle is not there. It is all guesswork. If the other man has to guess, why should I? Since I use the principle, I do not have to guess. I do not condemn any man's theory for it means nothing to me -for I will only use my own. Let him have his, but 

I will have mine."

"What is this principle you talk about? Is it some hidden secret?" "No Sir, it is not hidden from anyone; but man is afraid of it. Man has depreciated so far down the line that he has lost this greatest gift to man. 

As I look around this great institution, I think, 'what a crime against Creation.' When we are supposed to be molding men into real men, we are taking the real in the raw and molding it into the unreal. As I see it, tell these students who leave your school this year to face the disease of the human race, not one of them know more than the other. They have been bound, all of them, by one cord. If they as much as stretch that cord, they have instilled in them a fear and that is, they will lose the means of earning their three meals a day. 

Instead of turning men out, you turn out what once were men; but today, well I will leave you to be the judge, not I."

At this the eldest and most efficient of the professors got up and said, "Young man, as you go through life, you will find you will have a rough road to travel for the path you are on will be one man against the many. If you want to prosper, I would advise you to be careful how you speak your views." "All I can say is, you asked for it and my principle is, as the question is, so shall the answer be. 

Call for little, get little, call for much get much; so 

I would say to you, if you want the truth, ask for it. When it has been given, do not be offended. 

If you want anything but truth then hold your peace. 

I never open a conversation, neither do I ask for questions; but I am always ready to meet both?"

"Then as I see it," says the professor, "you will not use the methods taught you in this college." "Some, yes, and some, decidedly not; but I will answer my questions as the Law calls for them. 

After that, wait and see." "That is enough. You will, I am afraid, get yourself into trouble; but as you say, that is your business." "Yes Sir, and mine alone." 

As he sees his superior getting uneasy, he leaves him with a smile-not of victory but a sympathy for the man he has just left. "A wonderful mind, a big man, yet afraid of his position. If ever I have a position I am afraid of, I hope it may be taken from me the moment I have that fear."

Each day he goes through the routine and when he is called upon to illustrate his education, he goes through the task so easy that both students and professors marveled at his work. There is no loophole for dishonesty by the other man. Soon this will come to an end, he will become what his heart has desired as long as he can remember: A real friend to suffering humanity.

As the session goes on, the head of the college invites him more and more to spend the evenings with him. He becomes interested in this young, unusual man; a young man who is not afraid of anyone or anything; yet can talk on any subject that he, the professor, can bring up. Nothing seems to hold him down. When the young master leaves, after every conversation, the quiet thoughts of the Professor are: "What a body, but what a mind. I wonder how far he will go with this principle he talks of."

As the young man leaves the thought always comes, "I thought professors knew something, but I find they, too, just expound borrowed knowledge." 

Soon the session is over and his third year is finished. 

He leaves with all honors as he did in previous years and wonders how much of this routine would stay with him. "Two years more in some hospital.

Well, that will be more interesting than Education in actual use. I will come in contact with the man who should know something and the masses who think they do. I hope they, too, will not disappoint me."

He chooses a hospital with a high degree of efficiency. As he enters into this field of knowledge and experience, he is reserved to the point of silence for the first few weeks. He stands by and watches the play; but this time the play is suffering humanity and the actors are the men in charge of the hospital. 

They do not fail to let him know he is just an interne. For a while, he submits to their orders in silence and with a smile. He becomes the favorite in this hospital. He does not carry his head high, he does not try to look superior to his patients, he does his work quietly and gently, he does not hurt where it is not necessary, neither does he spare his patient where pain is necessary. He does not prolong anything. When he is there one year, he is able to talk and is asked for his opinion. When asked, he gives it without hesitation. As they begin to listen to him, they see he was not condemning any other man's method of treatments; but gives illustrations so clear and easy to understand the principle behind his talk that they could not help but see how much more superior he was. He describes

one disease after another to the head of the hospital. He also can see that in his institution he had an unusual mind. His time comes to an end and he is offered a position with a good salary to remain with this institution, but his reply to this was: "No, the field of labor is not big enough and again you would turn my efficiency into a money proposition; then I would not be working for the benefit of the sick but for the financial standing of the institution. 

If some poor person comes and wanted my services, they would be refused. I would make a name, but what is in a name? No, I want to be able to see rich and poor alike, I want to see them all on the same level-whether it be a high official or a laborer in my office, each will be a piece of suffering humanity. If they both have the same disease, then they both will be suffering the same pain; so they will both require the same treatment. To me, there are no great men-just men no matter who they are." "My young man, you have a wonderful Ideal in life. I cannot see failure in your future, but I can see being constantly in demand and little for your labor in return; for if you do not make a charge, you will find thousands who will work upon your sympathy." "Oh, I know that, but I do not need to accumulate wealth, I do not want it. I would rather re-lieve a poor suffering child and get nothing than relieve a rich man's child and get much. The first I would be doing with a bill in front of me as an incentive on the part of the people to try to get more out of me than I could give or a little more attention. If I should ever drift into this part of it, I

would not want to go on. I have another line of thought I know you will smile at. Why will you smile? Because you have been ordered to by your superiors; and since I have no superiors in the world of medicine, I have no one to take orders from. I am going to go into the principle of every art of healing, no matter what it is not with a biased mind to find fault or condemn the man who uses it, but I will watch the principle and every principle I find efficient I will uphold. Anyone I find does not come up to my standard, I will forget and let the law take care of it. I will not pass judgment on any field of the healing art because it might hinder my practice. Let the best man, the man who shows the most results of his labor and knowledge, win. If we as Medical Doctors, were as efficient as we should be, would the people leave us to try to find relief elsewhere? No, they would not have to. We fail in so many things that the masses are getting tired of it and seek relief. Can or do you blame them? If you, Professor, had a severe pain and you knew by your knowledge of your own art that nothing you could do would relieve that pain, but you gave some of your patients with that same pain medicine without results, and they found some man who had given them relief in a few minutes-would you look great and manly before the eyes of the people who know you, if you suffered on day after day just because you had been taught to not accept this man's theory? I should say not. If I knew such a man and told you about him and you refused to go to him for fear of what the people might say; then I would be apt to say, 'suffer on for your weakness-not your strength of character, but for your

unmanly weakness.' I know I would be speaking truth for I know if I had a pain that medicine could not reach, I would not submit to torture if I knew someone who could relieve me. I would be a fool, not a man, if I did. So, Sir, you see I could not stay in this institution; but I thank you for your consideration. My mind, my knowledge, my life is not for sale. There is no price high enough." "Young man, you talk as never man talked before. Where do you get this from?" "From a mind so much bigger than mine that it will take years for me to reach the goal he set before me." "Well, all I can say, 'you will get there.' As I see it, no matter what you say you leave no room for argument. No argument, no doubt; but I am sorry we cannot retain you. You surely have made your name here in the last two years."

He packs his belonging and leaves for home once more. "All I have to do now to become a Doctor in the eyes of the law is to meet a Medical Board and be questioned by them. I wonder how big their questions will be. Is there a man big enough outside of my Master-mind can ask a question that I could not answer? If there is, there is something wrong with me." His past life of advancements are all in his favor and in due season, he is before the Medical Board. 

His answers satisfy, so they admitted him to the field of Medicine and Surgery,-with all due honors. 

Some day our young man will reveal his examination. 

Again, he is in his home town and has an office arranged and enters into his life in real earnest; for

he is now entering and answering the call of his Soul-mind.

He becomes an efficient Doctor just as he had been a lawyer. His mind goes back to the past and the words of his Master-mind come to him, his first seventeen years of the life of have to and the results of his forced education, his breakdown, then the meeting of the strange man who brought him strength of body-what he terms as "Creative Healing." Then his first year as he sees and masters the principle, his feeling, a natural feeling comes over him that takes all fear from him, then his first year he spends in the factory and the different trades he masters. He says, "Yes, and if I had to go back to it again, it would be just as natural as it was five years ago." His year as a traveling salesman, he received in his mental vision the big men he met and how they looked to him. He smiles again and says, "Men, yes," but then his thoughts come to the position they have offered him. The following year as he meets and plays golf with all the biggest men in the town in which he lives, how easy it is to conquer them all. The first day in his office as a lawyer, his success there, and then the call of his Soul-mind coming in force to be what he is today-a Doctor?

No, only to go through the school of medicine to meet other men in this field. Have they come up to the standard? No, but it served the purpose. He was a Doctor by law and accepted as such, with still his open mind to look into other principles. Again he smiled and said, "Still I know nothing to what 

I want to know. I can see a bigger field ahead of me than this." As he is sitting letting these mental pictures pass before his mental vision, his door-bell

rings. He opens his door.-A client? No, someone seeking a position as secretary. He looks at this young girl and is satisfied with her. He gives her instructions. She will have to see every patient first and take the statements of the patient as follows: "How long have you been sick? Have you had a

Doctor before or if more than one, what were the names of the Doctors you visited? What did each of them find in their diagnosis? Did you get any benefit from the medicine you got from these Doctors? If so, how long did it last? Why are you changing Doctors? All these things I want you to take down. Write as the patient talks; and in their own original way, have them sign their names at the bottom of these pages." "Yes Sir, I worked in a Doctor's office, but," "Never mind the but. If you want to stay with me, follow my directions. One more order. No matter who comes in, show no partiality. The big and the small, the rich and the poor, the man with high position or the man who sweeps the streets are all on the same level, when they enter my office. I am from now on the servant of the masses.' "Yes, Sir." 

Patients begin to come, first one or two and he is satisfied. His results must be the only means of his practice. He has not been home more than a week or two when he enters his home and felt there is something wrong. His father comes downstairs with a sad expression on his face. When he asks him, "Why so sad?" he says, "your mother took very sick a few moments ago, but I knew you would be home soon so I did not call."

He goes to his mother's room and at the first

glance, he becomes alarmed at what he sees. She has a dangerous heart attack. He tells his father to call Dr. So & So and tell him to lose no time. 

In the meantime, he is trying to give his mother relief by the knowledge he has in the medical field; but nothing he can give her without harm seems to relieve her pain.

When the Doctor arrives, he, too, looks on for a moment and says, "All we can do is give her so and so."

The son says, "I have given her that without effect." "Then do you not think we should call in a specialist ?" "Yes, anything to save my mother."

The other Doctor suggests that he, the son, go to the office of the specialist and tell the symptoms so he might know better what to bring along with him to meet the emergencies of the case. As our young Master-mind is hurrying down the street, who does he meet but his Master-mind. He tells the story of his mother's illness. "Do not let me keep you, I will go to your home and wait for you." 

The specialist soon arrives and the three Doctors see the case. The specialist brings from his satchel some medicine. The young man is quick to see what it is. He stays the hand of the specialist and says, "Will that do good or harm? This is my mother." "Well, it is a chance. Sometimes, it works one way and sometimes another." "Then you do not know for sure?" "No, there is nothing sure in medicine. You should know that."

"I do. Here we are, three of us, and not one of us can relieve the suffering of my mother. What do we get at college anyway?"

At this, the specialist says, "Well, there is nothing in the world of medicine that can counteract this disease."

His mother is becoming unconscious. The three of them go downstairs together, two just physicians, the other the son of the sick-only he has the real interest at heart, to the other two, she is just a patient, a case of every day occurrence to them. As they pass the door, the Master-mind or the strange man hears the words, "Sorry, but there is nothing to save your mother." 

The young man bursts into tears.

His Master-mind comes to him and says, "Son dry your eyes, they speak without authority. There is something to save your mother and that is 'Creative Healing.' Have I not told you there is no limit to man's mind? Take me to her."

They enter the bedroom and the strange man sits by her bed. The young man stands at the foot of the bed. The strange man takes a small bottle from his pocket and the young man asks, "What is that you carry?" "Only a small bottle of olive oil to make a smooth rubbing surface."

He puts his hand under the cover and 'Creative 

Healing' begins.

In a few moments, the young Doctor sees his mother heave a sigh. Her eyes open and she looks around the room. She sees her son standing at the foot of the bed and says to him, "Son, I can breathe again. What a relief!" She turns to see who is sit-

ting at her bedside. "So you do fulfill your promise? 

You were needed this time." 

He strokes her brow and says, "A good sleep will do you good." Turning to the nurse who has been sent to care for her, "Let her sleep until she awakens of herself;" then turning to his young 

Master-mind, "another world of Wisdom shall be opened up to thee. Thou shall do many times in the future life what you have seen me to do today.

The medical power is really not to blame. They have been taught the material only, they know not of this wonderful limitless power in man's mind. 

They build a fence around their brain and whatever they can put within that fence is put there by education. They cannot see or speak anything that is beyond the fence. Someday, I hope you will take that fence down and give all young men who are inclined to help suffering humanity the principle of giving beyond the fence of education and enter the great expanse of the field of Wisdom-for only the wise should have the power to administer to the sick and suffering."

They sit and talk for hours; and the young Doctor takes his first lesson in this great power of healing that has surprised medical men for years. "Medicine will fail, has failed, that is a proven fact; but Creation, if given a fair chance, even after the Medical Doctors have given up in despair, will not fail. He, the Creative Healer, can and has stepped into the picture with a smile and says, 'Dry your tears, they will come back to you.'-Not in all cases, but a Creative Healer can see in a moment whether the patient can be brought back or not. He never gives a false hope, therefore, gains the confidence

of the people. As he speaks so do they believe. 

As you advance into this wonderful power, you will see many things you never saw before. You will know the cause and will be able to remove the cause in many diseases that cannot or have not been removed by medicine. I do not condemn neither the 

Doctor nor his medicine. Creative Healing is based on Wisdom, and if medicine can reach the disease then let medicine do so; but if it fails then why not use the last and greatest resort 'Creative Healing?' 

My Son, go forth on the continued circle of your life, that is, 'seek Wisdom that you might know all things, accumulate power to use the great unseen power for the benefit of the masses, cultivate a love for the suffering; and these three powers will surpass all other resources.' Your mother is awake. 

I hear her. We will go to her once more before I leave."

They go up to her room. The son sees a smile on his mother's face as she says, "What a sleep!"

The strange man once more puts his hand on her right side and keeps it there for a few minutes. He tells the young man to listen to her heart-beat. He does so and looks up as he says, "And will I be big enough to do for others even as you have done this for us?"

I am 

His Master-mind says, "Yes, you are young, old, you have the law on your side. Every time I do this, I am a law-breaker; but do you not think it is really an honor to be punished for deeds such as these?" "Sir, I would and will go through anything to reach the plane of life on which you live." "They say, "Time waits for no man, not even he

who creates;' and the night is late so I will take my leave. I want you to take a journey with me in two weeks. I have another young Master-mind I want you to meet." "What you say, that I will do. I will live in the world of anticipation of this journey."

As they walk to the door, the young Doctor says, "You have added another addition to my debt to you. How can I ever repay you?" "The first time you see a case such as your mother was and you use Creation and restore her to her husband and children, then that debt will be paid. 

Good Night. I will see you two weeks hence."

The two weeks seem long to our young Doctor; but at last the day arrives and so does his Mastermind. They meet at his office. As they leave, he tells his secretary to put a notice on the door and close the office. Turning to his master, he asks, "How long?" "One week. You need a week's rest." "As you say so it shall be."

They take the train, reach their destination in the morning, and take a taxi to the mountain where he, the Master-mind, had left just three days ago. As they near the top of the mountain the scenery is beautiful. The young man can hold his peace no longer, so he says, "This is the place I have seen so often in my mental vision." "Yes," his Master-mind smiles back to him, as he says, "I have watched you through life from these mountains. This is my sacred spot where I receive my revelations of all mysteries.

It is here, away from the noises of the world, that we can get in touch with the higher minds,

when I speak of higher minds, it may appear strange to you; but wait until you see the sunset upon this mountain then watch the moon rising, wait until you see a sunrise and see the moon go down into the lake. Yes, wait until you listen to the birds with their song of praise to their Creator and smell the sweetness of natural Creation. There are many things you will see on this mountain that will open your eyes as they were never opened before. Your ears will become so keen that the most silent whisper that floats through the air will be a level voice to you. Your bosom will, I know, swell with such emotion as you have never felt before. 

You will have to force nothing; but the vastness of the Universe as seen from the mountain will force you to express yourself. I have two missions here for you and a young man whom you will be proud to meet. When I left him three days ago, I told him of you, and I know he is now trying to see the roadway beyond his vision to see us coming. After the next turn in the road, we will be able to see him. 

There he is waving to us. What a wonderful young man he is; but not any more wonderful than the one 

I am taking to meet him." Soon the car stops and he, the other young man, steps forward to meet them. He, you can see, is full of a feeling that he can not express. He only says, "I am so glad to meet you. I have breakfast ordered and it will be ready in five minutes. We will go to breakfast first then talk afterwards."

This is all new to our young Doctor, a man who spends all his time with people, seeking and searching to find a principle of manhood; and here he sees a young man just as natural as himself. What a

find! As they walk together to the place where breakfast is waiting, the young man of Truth says, "So you, too, have taken the Path of Wisdom?" "Yes, but as I understand our paths, although they lead to the same goal, are in a different field. 

He told me your path is to bring Truth and Light to the world, mine is to bring to the world Creative Healing. As you advance the mind, I will be eliminating sufferings of humanity. We should be proud of such a Master." "We are, are we not?"

They soon leave for the mountain, and as they do, the young man of Truth says, "You will bear with me, but I feel so full;" and as he says these words, tears are in his eyes. He takes down his violin and smiling turns to his brother-in-wisdom and says, "If

I cannot find words, I speak my thoughts through my violin." He starts to play silently at first; then as he plays the volume increases and increases until you would think the strings would burst with vibration. 

He brings it to a close and turns to his Master-mind. 

As he does so, his Master-mind says, "I saw and understand, you have told me, although in music, yet I read it in words. Someday I will write the words for you, then you will know how beautiful your Soulmind gives its thanks to the Creator-not to me for I am just fulfilling the trust He gave me and I am passing that trust on to you that you might forward the truth of the Creator."

He smiles upon them with a smile that can come only from a satisfied Soul-mind. 

They spent the first day in a general conversation. 

As night comes, all three walk to the top of the mountain to watch the beauty of the Heavens and

to have a feast of silent reception from Creation. 

Not one speaks until the Master says, "Well, it is time we go down and rest for the night. I did not sleep well on the train last night. There is nothing like this coolness on the mountain to make you forget the world and all that is in it until morning."

CHAPTER XIV  

The next morning as our three leave for the mountain top, each one is it seems, under a silent mood. The two are in great anticipation for they know their life's work is going to be revealed unto them; and the vastness of Wisdom will be opened more unto them. As they reach their place of conversation and sit down, their master speaks for the first time since he has said "Good Morning." He seems to be looking into the future path each of these two young men had started so early in life. 

He goes over the ground they have already trayeled, one the author of the most wonderful book on the Truth of Creation and the other who had conquered all the biggest positions in the material life and sees the principle at its truest possible standard. 

He has proven to himself without a doubt that the natural can always cause the unnatural to retreat. 

He can fit into any position in life and make good. 

He will now enter into a new field, the greatest field known to the world-The field of disease to Humanity; and by this great Creative Power and his standing in the material world as his shield from the combination who will retard the progress of anyone who might be able to go beyond them and do things that they can not do. These are the thoughts that come before the mental picture of this Mastermind.

He begins by saying, "I have brought you two advanced reincarnated Soul-minds together today to lay the path you belong to once more before you, just as I have in many lives in the past. You see you are just a continuation really of one continuous life. You are two Soul-minds who saw and understood. You are two who showed your greatness even in your youth in this life. No one who saw you as children could look upon you without using these words: 'how bright and intelligent your child is.' 

You see the light of the Soul-mind shines through the eyes as soon as the child comes into the world. 

These things your parents will tell you. People talked; but as you advanced and tore petal after petal from your Soul-mind, you began to show your real beauty and spoke wonderful thoughts. The people were not surprised for they expected this of you; yet you went beyond all their anticipations. 

Now you are going further, you are going to take up life in its fullest meaning. The one will give 

Truth to the world. The truth you will give will change fear into respect, change a coward into a man, change the selfish into the unselfish, it will turn the world of thought at the present time into a new -not a new thought, but the thought our great and wonderful, our natural and loving, our majestic yet humble, yes, the Master who still today is the ideal of every mother in every country. -He who brought Truth in its fullest meaning; but was misunderstood as he talked to the masses. 

He was truly understood by the High Priests, Doctors and Ministers of the Law in his day. 

Yes, the masses did not see but the others saw and were afraid; so they cut His life of Truth and

greatness short. His truth and His life and His mission were put before the people in such a way that instead of what He spoke, manhood, they turned his life into the destruction of manhood. 

They tell us He wanted us to be humble, come to 

Him as little children, be penitent and cry for mercy. They tell us He said if we do not live as other men would have us live, we would be doomed forever. This makes us cowards not men. Now the only way," looking at his young Master-mind of Creation in Truth, "and it is your duty to reveal every mystery in this wonderful Creation. Do not leave one thing untold, from the beginning of this world even unto its end. Take it through every step of Evolution. Do not leave one thing out of it; and when you have done this the masses will see and understand in all Truth. You know your writing and speaking will be truth and nothing but truth; and as the people see the principle behind this wonderful Creation, their eyes will be opened. You will not condemn anything. No creed or religious denomination shall enter into your books. All we have to do is write even as the Great Silent Voice speaks to us; then we will not have to condemn. As the masses see the Truth, they, of their own free will, will change from the darkness to the light. They will see that the path they have been living in is just a false dream; and once they see the truth of

Life they will never go down to the land of fog again. It will not be necessary to have men praying and pleading and crying to the Creator to help us, we will see that it is us and us alone. It is the individual himself who must reach this goal in the Path of Manhood and Wisdom. It is then that the

masses will find there is a Power to be used by man -Not only a gift to the few, but to everyone who realize the greatness in his Creator, and realize that such a Creator would not give a special gift to any of us; for He said in the beginning, 'man, I give all of you a material brain; but within you I put a Soul so you might breathe and live. Within that soul

I put the greatest universal gift to all men alike; and that gift is the Soul-mind, the part of man that cannot be destroyed.—This is part of me; so it is part of Creation and cannot be destroyed. It will go on and on as long as time goes for is it not my gift? As you advance this Soul-mind, so will you fulfill the trust I put in you.' All these things the Silent Voice will speak to you as you go along the Path of Truth. Know there is no limit to your 

Soul-mind. I would say 'whatsoever, you hear from this Silent Voice, whatsoever you shall read from the book of Creation, the book that the Creator only could be the author, listen, read and give.'- 

The more you give, the more you will receive. The fountain of Wisdom will never run dry; for if you come to the point where there is not a mystery left in the Earth on which you live, then there is the 

Universe as far as you can see. In due season they will speak to you as Creation speaks, with unquestionable truth. The Creator saw in his Creation that nothing could lie but man. He saw in the end that man would, sometime, come to the life of Truth also. Each man will then know from where he came, why he is here and where he is going. The mystery of this thing we call 'Death' will be truthfully understood by all His supreme Creation. Your future is a great one. You are a man alone at present; but

as you give or sow the seed of Truth, the harvest in turn will be worth the sowing. I say, 'sow thy seed quietly and the people will come into your Circle of Truth in their silent way.' Speak not Truth from the house-tops, expound not thyself in public places nor try to become elevated above thy fellowman. Live not as the masses, but be big enough to live with them—not as one of them, but as a Light among them. Let thy truth be seen and felt-not heard by noises." Turning to him, he says, "Are you ready to fulfill this trust the Creator put in thee? If so, the field of labor is ready for thee.

The masses know there is something they do not understand; yet they are expecting a Dawn of a 

New Era. You are the center of the New Dawn. 

Go forth and let your light shine. Your reward will be given unto you each day by the results of your labor." "Sir, I am ready-no matter what I have to go through, no matter what I am called upon to bear, I will go on advancing to the end of time." 99 "Then,' says the Master-mind, turning to the other young Master-mind, "your field of labor is vast. I hear the cries of Suffering Humanity saying, 'come and help us, we seek help but no help comes. 

What can we do? We try everything, but with no relief. Still mothers are taken from their children and fathers are taken or are forced into poverty by laying in their bed of sickness or pain.' Only Creative Healing can meet this cry of distress. Only through this power that was used by the greatest mind of all men in the history of men can the sick and suffering be relieved. Other methods have done much, but not enough. After years of research,

there are many diseases that have never been reached. Creative Healing can and has stood the test. Did the Master of all men work miracles? -No. In the eyes of the people? Yes; but in the eyes of the man of Wisdom, No. He used the great power of Creation in all His work-Not to show the masses His greatness, but to relieve the suffering. That was the principle behind His power, for as He saw so it was, as we see so it will be. He did not heal to reach the field of fame. He did not heal that His name might be spoken to others. He healed only the individual. He was all Power and 

Love for Humanity. There is no limit to this power; yet it is based upon the utmost common sense, and will only be used when the principle behind it is clean and not for the gain from mercenary value that might be put upon it by others. As this great true wonderful principle is revealed unto thee, you will see that it is so far beyond human comprehension, yet so sure and easy that you will not only want to use it for others but you will want to teach others. When others try to persecute you, you will not get angry toward them; but will keep silent and not condemn their attitude, knowing that they are only of the material and think of them as such. Man cannot see as we see for they have been blinded by circumstances of life. No one knows any more of this part of life than you. That is why I wished you to go through the material with an open mind so when the time comes for you to speak you will have a clear knowledge of both sides of the picture. You will have truth in the material way and truth in the way of Creative Healing. 

You will in time have the masses in your favor and

when they see as you and I want them to see, they will realize it is a most wonderful step forward in the path of Evolution. Yes just another step to prevent sickness and sorrow and pain. What a principle in life to live for! Remember there is no limit to the height to which you can go, no limit to the field of labor you have entered; so I would say to you that 

I have no fear for you. I have watched you through life." "Yes," says the young man, "and put me in debt so far that nothing short of a life for the field I have chosen can pay that debt. I will fulfill your trust, no matter what I have to go through or how I may have to suffer. Did not our Master suffer? As He did so will I, if it be put upon me." 99 "Son, you make me swell with thanks to my Creator for the path He made it possible for me to find-The Path of Wisdom, Power and Love, the three greatest words that were ever conceived by the mind of man. A Wisdom so vast it knows no bounds, a Power so great you never can take all its resources, a Love so wonderful that it is as a fountain of clear water-a water you may drink and drink and wish your thirst would never be quenched. After all we can put everything into few words: Education can only come from the fountain of Knowledge-Knowledge can only come from the fountain of Wisdom-Wisdom can only come from 

Creation-And Creation can only come from the Creator."

After these words, the Master-mind arises and says, "What I have said, I have said; and as the

Silent Voice spoke, I spoke, therefore, receive and contain-but before I finish, I would have you know:

You have Light and Truth, Give to those who would have it; 

But sow not your seeds on rocks thorns or hard trodden ground. Waste not your seed, it is too precious."

READ the next two books: 

Creation in Truth by the life of Truth, and

Creative Healing and its Possibilities. 

by

JOSEPH B. STEPHENSON Master of Creative Healing.

NOTE 

As you read my book, The Human Rose Bud, I know you will say, "We have never had such men live;" and to a certain degree, I agree with you. 

As sure as this book is written from the Book of Wisdom, it is giving a true picture of our young men of (yesterday)-No. Of our young men of today?—I am sorry to again say No; but of our young men of tomorrow. As sure as Creation is real, as sure as the world is, as sure as the Creator saw, so sure will these young men live; for what He saw must be and without men such as these the world of men could not advance. 

Advancement can only come from advanced thought; advanced thought brings advanced truth and truth will bring manliness; so it will come. 

Look to your child and as you see so give your son a chance in life.

A high Soul-mind may be subdued for a while; but will come forth of itself, therefore, I see a new world of new men as time goes on. 

A man of Wisdom only can open the eyes of the masses.

I have been asked by many, "Why do I have so much Wisdom?" My answer is: "All the world is seeking after gold so that leaves all the Wisdom for me.' 99 

You can have riches; but give me Wisdom. 

The author 

J. B. S.
